15 Best Bedroom Lighting Ideas for a Relaxing Ambience (2026)

📅 Sep 08, 2021  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

When you’re trying to unwind at the end of a long day, turning on the “big light” is simply not the move. The glare of a way-too-bright overhead light is not only unflattering but oftentimes overstimulating. It can make your otherwise cozy bedroom feel more like a department-store dressing room, leaving relaxation a little bit harder to come by. The right bedroom lighting, on the other hand, can bring a sense of warmth, atmosphere, and visual interest to the space where you spend some of your most cherished downtime.

The secret lies in layered lighting, which involves a series of soft illumination sources working together in concert: A bedside lamp provides focused task lighting for reading, while wall sconces free up valuable nightstand space and introduce a soft diffused glow. Floor lamps can brighten an otherwise neglected corner, and a beautiful chandelier or pendant light doubles as a bedroom’s focal point, even when the light is switched off.

Dimmable fixtures and warm white light bulbs can be especially transformational in bedrooms that also function as an office or a hangout space. Ahead, we’ve rounded up our favorite bedroom lighting ideas, including sculptural statement pieces, portable table lamps, and wall-mounted fixtures that usher in a sense of calm.

For a reading nook, a work-from-home space, or just a little warm white lighting, a floor lamp can show off your taste without sacrificing functionality. We’ve pulled two designer-favorite must-haves for those in the world of interior design.

This 1960s model from Danish designer Mads Caprani hasn’t gone out of style since its release. The pleated shade, bentwood spine, and U-shaped cast-iron base make it one of the most easily recognized and iconic designs in floor lamps to this day. If you’re looking to make a statement about your style, this piece does just that—landing somewhere between studious and stylish, as in polo champion Nacho Figueras’s Florida home.

Another lamp for tastemakers, this design from Isamu Noguchi has made the rounds in many celebrity home tours from a minimalist Brooklyn brownstone to Troye Sivan’s modern Melbourne home. Commerce director Rachel Fletcher has previously named a Noguchi lamp one of her bedroom essentials. The reputation of Noguchi’s work and the unique style of his Akari lamps make this a design staple for anyone hoping to cultivate a modern bedroom.

We love the look of dual table lamps to frame your bed and create a distinct visual space in your bedroom. It also allows your bed to feel like a conscious design choice instead of a strictly functional piece. We’re taking bedside lamp notes from this Portland home owned by the lead singer of The Shins and decorated by Jessica Helgerson. And, if you don’t want to do things matchy-matchy, any one of these lamps would look great solo too.

We love this moody metallic option from Article for a midcentury look—and we named it one of our favorite bedside lamps after ample editor testing. At $149, the price is also right.

If you’re struggling to find a table lamp that plays well with others, this is a viable option from coastal core experts Serena & Lily. This comes in four beautiful shades, inspired by the Italian shoreline, and there’s even a touch of rattan for a beachy bedside table moment.

The Flos Bellhop table lamp is a great source of ambient light in this minimalist Barcelona bedroom.

A godsend for a bedroom with inconvenient outlets, these design-forward portable lamps bring both ambience and task lighting wherever you need them.

Another favorite of interior designers, the Flos Bellhop table lamp is a great source of ambient light in a minimalist setting. These super-portable LED lamps have four dimmer settings, are USB-compatible, and can last up to 24 hours on a single charge. And, if you love the simple glass shape, they’re also available as wall sconces for a stylish outdoor lighting moment.

You never know when you’ll need a little extra light, and the Poldina Pro can adapt to your ever-changing needs. The lamp pairs a petite silhouette with the clean lines of Italian minimalist design, making it a versatile fit for your nightstand, dresser, or reading nook. Without any unsightly wires to speak of, the lamp can appear as an intentional design choice just about anywhere. Its touch-sensitive dimmer lets you adjust the brightness and choose between three color temperatures.

AD shopping writer Julia Harrison, who tested the Micro version, appreciates its versatility for desk work and kitchen prep alike. She notes that the lamp “blends shyly and seamlessly into all setups that require a little, but not too much, light.” With approximately 12 hours of battery life, it’s an especially convenient option for adding a little extra illumination wherever you need it.

Wall lighting brings a warm dimension to the design of your bedroom and provides great task lighting for a bedtime read. While we love table lamps on matching nightstands, wall sconces bring an added degree of intentionality in their design, as seen in Walton Goggins’s guest bedroom and Jennifer Garner’s kid’s bedroom.

Available in 24 colors and with flexible orientation, this wall sconce channels the fun throwback design sensibility of Portland-based Schoolhouse. Not relegated to the bedroom alone, these fixtures can bring vibrancy to any space—as is evident in the bathroom of this timeless, edgy Massachusetts home.

A visible mending detail modernizes a traditional style, so the pleated lampshade remains classic without being too precious. We love to imagine this lining a long entryway, or on either side of a tall headboard—the perfect lighting for bedtime pages.

Linen shades are classic for a reason: They soften the appearance of a light fixture, giving even the most straightforward silhouettes a dash of warmth and character. Quince’s Contemporary Sconce pairs crisp white linen shades with a streamlined steel frame, available in brass or black for two distinctly different looks. The brass finish befits a more traditional bedroom, while the black feels especially sharp juxtaposed against pale walls and minimalist decor. Choose a single or double configuration depending on your space, or install a matching pair on either side of the bed for a polished alternative to bedside table lamps.

If a flower could moonlight as a light fixture, it would probably look something like Soho Home’s Florie Wall Light. Inspired by the interiors of White City House, the members’ club in West London, the sculptural design features two gently curved layers of champagne-colored glass that resemble a tulip’s petals. A fluted, bubbled texture gives the glass an almost vintage quality, while the frosted finish diffuses the light into a soft, atmospheric glow. Even at just nine inches tall and nine inches wide, the fixture has enough personality to stand on its own, though opting for a pair to flank the bed would be an especially charming choice to imbue the space with a dash of romance.

Actress Laura Harrier hangs a bespoke pendant lamp in her bedroom.

The wrong kind of flush mount can be an eyesore, and the right kind of pendant light can transform a space from tragic to tasteful. These picks from IKEA and Lumens are both contemporary yet classic, proving them a satisfying finishing touch that makes all the difference.

IKEA brings an affordable yet still-elevated style to the lineup with this ubiquitous, rattan-like pendant (technically latticed bamboo) from Ilse Crawford. We love this style as much for dining room lighting as we do for bedroom lighting. Can’t decide which? Two will only cost you $140 total.

Danish lighting design has a knack for making even the most practical objects feel like works of art. Case in point: the PH 5 Hanging Lamp. Its distinctive arrangement of overlapping shades creates a sculptural silhouette, with each layer directing and diffusing light. The three-shade system sends most of the illumination downward while allowing a softer glow to escape around the sides for ambient lighting. The result is a striking pendant that commands attention, even when it’s not turned on. The light would look especially beautiful suspended above a bedside table in place of a traditional lamp.

Supermodel Elsa Hosk incorporated a pink Tronchi chandelier, made of Murano glass, into her New York City bedroom.

Might some consider a chandelier too much for a bedroom? Yes, someone who needs to live a little. A chandelier brings a sense of occasion to every space it’s in, and your bedroom deserves no less. These picks bring elegance without overkill.

We love how supermodel Elsa Hosk incorporated a pink Tronchi chandelier made of Murano glass into her New York City bedroom, and you can find a similar pick on 1stDibs if you want to sleep in rose-colored bliss.

Often referred to as the “boob light,” this type of flush mount can get a bad rap, but West Elm has figured out how to make them shine. This contemporary ceiling light fixture is simple, aesthetic, and subtle.

Serena & Lily’s Capiz Scalloped Chandelier has the delicate charm of a seaside find, with a little extra glamour courtesy of its antique-gold framing. Each translucent capiz shell is individually hand-set, creating a softly scalloped silhouette that feels like a piece of jewelry for the ceiling. The natural variations in the shells’ shapes and opacity give the fixture a beautiful sense of movement. Light filters through the overlapping layers to cast a warm diffused glow, making it an especially lovely choice for a bedroom where atmosphere matters just as much as illumination.

If you’re looking for guidance when it comes to your bedroom lights, it’s important to keep a variety of factors in mind. Do you do a lot of reading in bed? Maybe table lamps should be your primary focus. Do you have enough fixtures to keep the space illuminated but not too bright? Perhaps a floor lamp or two will provide that glow for you. Are you spending most of your time winding down and heading to sleep? Try a night-light or string lights for ambience! And, of course, you’ll want multiple light sources to get the look (and the vibe) just right. But don’t fret, we’ve combed the AD home tour archives—and shopping lists—for the best bedroom lights around.

Cranberry Pie

📅 Aug 27, 2021  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

What People Are Saying:“I made 3 desserts this Thanksgiving and this was an absolute hit!! Everyone at the table raved about it including my super picky 7 year old who had herself seconds. Definitely a winner and will be making again.” - PurplePuzzle

Preheat your oven to 350° and grease a 9-inch pie plate with butter.

For the crust, grab a large bowl and mix together the Biscoff crumbs, melted butter, and a pinch of salt. Gently press this mixture into the bottom and up the sides of your greased pie plate.

Bake the crust until it turns golden and feels firm to the touch. Once done, set it aside on a wire rack to cool.

In a small bowl, mix the gelatin with some cold water and let it sit so it can bloom.

Next, for the filling, grab a medium saucepan, toss in the cranberries, brown sugar, orange juice, and zest, and bring it to a simmer over medium heat. Stir occasionally while the cranberries burst and the liquid has thickened slightly, which should take about 7 minutes.

Pour in the coconut milk, ginger, heavy cream, almond extract, and a little salt. Stir everything together and heat until it’s nice and steamy, but not quite simmering. Remove from the heat and stir in the bloomed gelatin until fully dissolved.

Now, blend the cranberry mixture until it’s super smooth—use whatever blender or food processor you have handy. Strain it into a bowl, toss out any solids, and let the mixture cool down to room temperature. Pour the cranberry filling into your crust and refrigerate until set, which should take at least 6 hours.

Serve with homemade whipped cream and enjoy!

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

This pie will keep for up to a week in the fridge and frozen for up to 5 months. Simply thaw it out in the fridge the next day and serve.

8 Best Travel Adapters (2026): 20+ Models Tested

📅 Jul 27, 2021  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Whether planning a country-hopping odyssey or a quick business trip, your journey will go more smoothly with the best travel adapters that allow you to safely charge all your gadgets wherever you land. After many work trips and family vacations I’ve tested several—both universal and basic options—and my favorites will work in most parts of the world.

The best adapters make great gifts for travelers and deserve a place in your carry-on, but check out our other travel guides, like the Best Travel Essentials, Best Travel Tech for Families, Best Travel Pillows, and Best USB-C Cables.

Updated September 2026: I added some Epicka travel adapters, a mention for a Tessan adapter, removed some older, unavailable adapters, and updated prices.

There’s a ton to like about this 100-watt universal travel adapter from reliable travel brand Ceptics. It covers more than 200 countries and can charge multiple gadgets in a hurry. The classic slider design pushes out EU, UK, and US plugs, and you can rotate the pins for sockets in Australia and China. I love how securely the plugs lock into place; you must press down firmly to slide them back. You have a universal input socket, an additional side socket for US or Japan plugs, and two USB-C ports and two USB-A ports on the bottom. But what makes this travel adapter my favorite is the retractable USB-C cable. It’s around 25 inches long, the end sticks to the adapter magnetically, and it saves you from having to pack an extra cord. The cable can deliver up to 100 watts in total. The USB-C ports support up to 15 watts, and the USB-A ports go up to 12 watts.

For all of its strengths, though, it does provide an unfortunate lack of any grounding. Grounding is meant to reduce the risk of an electric shock if there’s a fault, such as a loose wire inside a device with a metal case. (This video explains grounding well.) Thankfully, there’s overload protection with a 10-amp fuse, and it also comes with a spare. But you should not use this adapter with any device with a third metal ground pin on its plug.

I also tested the 140-watt Ceptics 6-in-1 model ($41), which omits the cable but has three USB-C ports and one USB-A, and the excellent 70-watt model ($40), which has the retractable cable but fewer ports and loses the extra outlet. Both are great alternatives.

Perhaps the smallest travel adapter that's still practical to use, the Epicka Air measures 3.46 x 1.97 inches and is just 1.19 inches thick, with the prongs folded away. It’s easy to pack but still feels reasonably sturdy in use. You get one USB-A and three USB-C ports. The first three ports offer up to 15 watts apiece, and the last USB-C port on the right can go up to 65 watts. But 65 watts is also the maximum output across the four ports, so it gets divided if you plug in multiple devices. But if you use the AC outlet too, and you can charge up to five devices at once.

When I first tested the 40-watt version ($27), I wasn’t sure about this travel adapter, but after using the 65-watt model for a while, I’ve grown to like it. I still think this style of folding design can be a bit fiddly to use, and the prongs sometimes fold back if you bump them while plugging in. But the placement of the EU prongs in the middle helps it stay plugged in more reliably than some larger sliding cube adapters; the US prongs lock into place, and I didn’t have any issues with the UK prongs.

This budget Ceptics device proves that you don’t need to spend that much money to snag one of the best travel adapters. It covers more than 150 countries, with sliders you push to reveal the three most common international plugs—EU, UK, and US. You can rotate the pins for sockets in Australia or China. The sliders lock into place securely, and there’s a button on the side for retracting them. This plug’s universal outlet can recharge your power-hungry devices, like laptops and camera batteries. Two USB-C and two USB-A ports on the bottom, plus a USB-C port on the side, can handle phones, tablets, smartwatches, and Kindles. Each USB-C port can deliver up to 15 watts, and each USB-A port up to 12 watts. While it's not as fast as some of my picks above, you won't care if you tend to charge your gadgets overnight.

There’s no grounding, so don’t plug devices with a third metal ground pin into this adapter. There is an 8-amp fuse for safety, and it comes with a spare. It is also certified by RoHS and CE.

Universal adapters don't always work properly everywhere, and most don’t have grounding. The cheaper and more reliable alternative is to buy individual adapters. The 12-piece international set from Ceptics is my favorite. It includes types to cover most of the globe, and each is properly grounded. Only visiting a single destination? Just pack the relevant type. These adapters don't have USB ports, so you’ll need the respective charging adapter for your gadgets.

This set is especially good for Europe because it includes Type C, E/F, and G adapters, so you’re covered for any socket. Ceptics offers an even cheaper and smaller five-piece set, but I don’t recommend it. The adapters are flimsy, there’s no grounding, and the European plug’s design doesn’t work well with the recessed sockets you will sometimes encounter. The drawback of plug adapters is that they’re easy to lose, but at least Ceptics includes a small bag for storage.

What I love about this sturdy, compact plug adapter is the lack of sliding pins or moving parts to worry about. It stays put in the outlet. Offered in several versions (Type C, Type E/F, Type G), this adapter enables you to charge up to six gadgets. I’ve been testing the Type G model in Scotland, and it plugs into a single outlet, giving you three US outlets, a USB-A port, and two USB-C ports. The USB-C can output up to 15 watts, and the USB-A ports are limited to 12 watts—not especially speedy, but fine for overnight charging. The adapter is grounded and has various safety features.

On the downside, it can be tough to use all the outlets simultaneously, as larger plugs, like the MacBook power adapter, tend to block one of the other US outlets. It does not offer surge protection, and the Type G adapter maxes out at 3,250 watts (250 volts, 13 amps), while the EU and other versions top out at 2,500 watts.

This kit includes multiple plug attachments that slide and lock into place to keep you powered across most of the world. It is bulky and much bigger than the average adapter but provides two grounded US AC outlets. There is also a built-in USB-C cable, two USB-A ports, and two USB-C ports. The USB-A ports and built-in USB-C cable offer a combined maximum charging rate of 15 watts. The USB-C ports support Power Delivery, with up to 20 watts each. The top charging rate for all of them simultaneously is 55 watts.

There is built-in surge protection, but since this is not a voltage converter, don’t plug in hair dryers or other non-dual-voltage electronics. While the two AC outlets are handy, their proximity means that the shape and size of some plugs can make it tricky to use both simultaneously. But with the ability to charge up to seven devices, this kit is handy. I also like the original Ceptics World Travel Adapter Kit ($35), though it lacks the two USB-C ports.

Super compact when folded flat, Anker’s MagGo 3-in-1 can charge your iPhone or Qi2-certified phone at up to 15 watts, with a second pad for AirPods or other wireless earbuds, and a fold-out Apple Watch charger at the back. You get a USB-C cable (around 5 feet) and a wall adapter with it, but pair it with something above that can provide at least 40 watts, and you can leave the wall adapter at home.

Only MagSafe iPhones and Qi2-certified Android phones have the magnets needed to use this as a stand, but I had no trouble charging an older Android phone by folding it flat (Qi2 is backward compatible). It does feel a little crowded when charging three devices, but the adapter nevertheless enables you to travel light. I recommend this Anker charger for travelers in our Best Apple 3-in-1 Travel Chargers guide.

There are 15 plug types in use across the world. Universal adapters tend to cover all of these types.

Some countries are not usually covered by universal adapters, such as India (Type D), Israel (Type H), and South Africa (Type M or N). You'll need to buy specific plug adapters for those places. To avoid any surprises when you land, double-check what type you need before you travel.

If you're visiting just one destination, a basic plug adapter that caters to one plug type is all you need. For trips to multiple destinations or for frequent flyers, a universal travel adapter can prove more versatile. The universal adapters I recommend here have the bonus of including multiple USB ports for charging several mobile devices from a single outlet.

Voltage converters are big, heavy, and expensive, and they don’t always work—so it’s probably best to avoid them. The reason you might think you need one is that the AC sockets on all of our recommended travel adapters do not convert the voltage coming from the socket. This means plugging into a UK socket will deliver 220 volts at 50 hertz, which is very different from the 120 volts at 60 hertz you can expect in the US. Don't worry! Your gear won’t get fried. You just need to make sure anything you plug into one of these universal travel adapters has something like this printed on it:

That should include most modern gadgets. If your device or charger can’t handle a variable voltage, it’s probably best to leave it at home. Most places provide hair dryers, irons, and kettles, so there’s no need to take them with you. It’s also often cheaper to buy a set with the correct plug at your destination and save the luggage space and hassle.

If you are determined to try a voltage converter (again, I don’t recommend doing so), the Ceptics 2,000-watt Travel Voltage Converter ($70) seems to work well. It has a special 2,000-watt outlet for hair dryers, but only ones that work via a mechanical switch (anything with an electronic circuit board for automatic switch-off or temperature control won’t work and is at risk of being fried if plugged in). It also has two outlets that go up to 200 watts, one USB-C port, and three USB-A ports, though the ports do not support fast charging.

Travel adapters and chargers usually state the wattage they can deliver for USB-C and USB-A ports, retractable cables, and wireless charging. If you're interested in getting the fastest rates possible, you’ll want a charger and cable that can deliver the maximum wattage to your device. A laptop might want 100 watts, while a phone only needs 30 watts. There's no need to worry if the charger can deliver more wattage than is required because the device will dictate how much power to draw. If the wattage is lower than required, most devices will simply charge slowly. You can find out more about charging standards like Power Delivery (PD), Quick Charge (QC), and others in my guide on how to fast charge your phone.

Yes, you can pack travel adapters in your suitcase. It may be wise to bring one in your hand luggage for easy access should you need to charge up a device when you arrive at your destination. In fact, devices like power banks with lithium batteries (must be capped at 27,000 mAh) must go in hand luggage, not checked bags.

Can I Buy Travel Adapters at the Airport or Hotel?

You certainly can buy travel adapters at the airport, but like all other goods you buy at kiosks between connecting flights, they will be far more expensive than necessary, and your choice will be limited.

Some hotels have travel adapters, and some even have wall outlets for other countries (or USB-A and USB-C ports), but most do not, so don’t bank on it. You can always check with your hotel or accommodation before you depart, but it’s safer to bring a travel adapter with you.

Should You Test Your Travel Adapter Before You Go?

Seriously, nothing is worse than thinking you’re being very clever by packing light only to get to your hotel and discover that your gadgets refuse to charge. Between the adapter, cable, and your device, plenty can go wrong, so do a quick test on your wall outlet at home with everything you are taking before you set off on an international trip.

Anytime I fly for work or vacation, I take a few travel adapters and chargers with me to test. I also test them for longer periods at home in the UK. I have power stations and power strips with US and EU outlets to test those plugs. I test each charger with a variety of devices (iPhone, Pixel, iPad, MacBook, AirPods). I try charging with every port and outlet simultaneously to see if the charger gets warm. I assess the charging rates, durability, size, weight, and usability of each device. If there are any special features, I test them too.

There are many travel adapters out there. These are a few others I tested and liked, but they missed out on a place above for one reason or another.

Epicka Pulse for $27: The beveled edges of this travel adapter from Epicka make it more comfortable to hold. The sliders work well and lock in place securely to cover you in around 200 countries. A pair of USB-C and two USB-A ports provide a good mix of port options. This adapter can deliver up to 45 watts from either USB-C port, while the USB-A ports top out at 18 watts. I also tested the newer Epicka Pulse Duo ($130), which can deliver up to 140 watts across three USB-C and one USB-A ports, has an extra US or Japanese socket in the side and boasts a smart display that shows you the current charging status, wattage, and temperature. Cool as it is, it's overkill for most folks and kinda pricey.

Tessan Travel Adapter 009 for $17: This is a similar design to the Epicka Air I recommend above, but the prongs don’t stay in place as securely when unfolded, and you can only draw a maximum of 20 watts from the one USB-A and two USB-C ports. But it is affordable and compact. Tessan also makes good, traditional, slider universal adapters: There's a 140-watt model ($70), and the most powerful travel adapter I’ve tested, the Voyager 205 ($128), which has six USB-C ports and one USB-A to deliver up to 205 watts. If you don’t care about charging speed, Tessan's 20W model ($29) is very reasonably priced and offers two USB-A and two USB-C ports in a small package.

UGreen Travel Plug Adapter for £17: This travel adapter is for UK folks visiting Europe, and it’s a solid option with a fixed plug (no mechanism). It has only two USB-A and one USB-C port for smaller devices, and it’s very bulky. I prefer the OneBeat above for this type of design.

Statik SmartCharge Pro for $80: This is a nice combo device that includes a 15,000 mAh power bank and a 60-watt wall charger. There are fold-out US prongs and slide-on adapters for the UK, EU, and AU. You also get one USB-A port, one USB-C port, a fold-out USB-C cable, and a wireless charging pad on top that works with your iPhone or Apple Watch.

D-Link 65W Multi-Port GaN Charger (DCP-651) for $40: If two USB-C ports and a USB-A port are enough for your travel needs, this affordable D-Link charger could be for you. It comes with interchangeable plugs for different countries. The 65-watt limit is enough for overnight charging a laptop and a couple of smartphones.

Arsmel VisaPro All-in-One Travel Adapter for $80: This chunky power adapter can charge laptops or multiple small devices with a whopping 170-watt maximum output. It works with any outlet in the US, UK, or EU, thanks to sliding plugs that slot in securely, and you can spin the prongs on the US plug to support the total number of countries (more than 200). I love the mecha-inspired paint job, too. There are four ports along the bottom: two USB-C ports rated at 140 watts, with the third USB-C and USB-A ports rated at 18 watts. It supports PD 3.1, QC 3.0, PPS, FCP, and SCP, and has a 10-amp fuse.

Satechi GaN Travel Charger for $120: If you only take small gadgets with you when you travel, a compact, multi-port wall charger is all you need and a great way to pack light. It plugs directly into the wall and comes with four interchangeable travel adapters for the EU, UK, and Australia, plus a mesh bag to store them. There are four USB-C ports that you can plug your gadgets into, including laptops like the MacBook Pro, tablets, phones, and wearables. With support for Power Delivery 3.1, the first two ports can supply up to 140 watts, and the bottom two ports up to 45 watts. However, the maximum output when all four ports are in use is 145 watts.

Baseus 70W Universal Travel Adapter With Retractable Cable for $40: This excellent travel adapter is almost identical to our top pick from Ceptics, offering up to 70 watts, sliding prongs to cover more than 200 countries, and a built-in retractable USB-C cable, not to mention a USB-C port and two USB-A ports. It also has a snazzy black-and-yellow finish. It only misses out because it’s slightly more expensive, but if you can snag one on sale or prefer the colors, it’s a solid option.

Epicka Universal Travel Adapter for $20: This travel adapter from Epicka is still a solid option. The sliding plugs cover 150 countries, and there are four USB-A ports on the bottom and a 15-watt USB-C port on the side. There’s also an 8-amp fuse with a spare, and this adapter is RoHS-, CE-, and FCC-certified.

Masterplug Visitor to UK Travel Adapter (3 Pack) for £12: For visitors to the UK, this simple trio of adapters accepts European, American, Australian, and Asian plugs. They are single-outlet adapters with 13-amp fuses inside, and they work perfectly. These are the adapters I use for long-term testing of devices from the US and elsewhere that lack UK plugs. If you forgot to buy an adapter before arriving in the UK, you can find this affordable three-pack at any Argos store.

Anker European Travel Plug Adapter for $16: One of our favorite portable charger brands, Anker, also makes travel adapters. This one plugs into most European outlets to provide a single grounded US outlet, a USB-A port, and two USB-C ports. Unfortunately, the three USB ports max out at 15 watts total, so it’s best for overnight gadget charging, but I appreciate the temperature and overload protection. There is also a UK version ($16).

Epicka Hybrid European Travel Plug Adapter for $20: This handy adapter turns one outlet into four and includes four USB ports (two A and two C). It has fold-out Type A prongs, but you can also slide on a fold-out Type C plug for use in Europe. While the USB ports support QC 3.0 or PD 3.0, they max out at 20 watts in total. I prefer the OneBeat adapters recommended above.

You can do better than these travel adapters.

EZQuest WorldTravel GaN 5 Port With PD Wall Charger: This travel adapter has a generous four USB-C ports and one USB-A port with a familiar slide-out prong design that covers more than 150 countries. There’s also a built-in 10-amp fuse and a spare. It has RoHS, CE, and FCC certification. It works fine, but it's kinda boxy, and the sliders don't lock in place as securely as some of our other picks. There's also a 65W version ($60).

Rolling Square Pocket Travel Adapter: I admire any attempt to shrink down travel adapters in size, but the three-pronged UK plugs are usually the obstacle. Rolling Square’s solution is to have a flip-out third prong, but you’ll need a good fingernail to unhitch it. This charger has a single USB-C port capable of delivering up to 30 watts and is a compact option that covers the US, UK, and Europe.

Ceptics 65W World Travel Adapter: This versatile adapter comes with Type A, B, C, E/F, G, and I slide-on plugs. They fit onto the rectangular brick and plug directly into the outlet, or you can attach them to the optional five-foot cable. The brick has one grounded US outlet and a second non-grounded outlet, with one USB-A (30-watt) and two USB-C (65- and 33-watt) ports at the other end. It works fine, and it's affordable, but it's bulky and ugly.

40 Gift Ideas for Your Sister-in-Law to Get on Her Good Side

📅 May 14, 2021  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Foolproof strategy: buying her affection!

Gilmore Girls: Rory And Dean's Relationship Timeline, Season By Season

📅 May 13, 2021  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The pilot of Gilmore Girls reveals Rory Gilmore (Alexis Bledel) as a sweet teenage girl who dreams of getting into Harvard, but it also marks the start of Rory and Dean's relationship timeline. She's leaving her small-town high school behind when she meets new kid Dean Forester (Jared Padalecki). Suddenly, she wonders if she should go to the prestigious private school Chilton after all, which starts a big fight between her and her cool mom, Lorelai (Lauren Graham). While Rory's romances become a point of contention throughout the series, her first relationship with Dean is the catalyst for a lot of the shifts in the show's early dynamics.

The foundation of Gilmore Girls is the relationship between Rory and Lorelai, but the romantic entanglements in the series play a big part in Rory's coming of age and Lorelai's ability to allow her daughter to spread her wings. Gilmore Girls deals with teenage life well and part of that includes Rory and Dean's relationship. From their meeting to the early days of their romance, along with their two break-ups and reunions later on in their lives, this love story is an important part of the show. Whether viewers are Team Dean or not, Rory's first big relationship on Gilmore Girls is hugely momentous.

Rory and Dean's relationship timeline begins in the very first episode of the series, unlike Rory's connections to other characters in Gilmore Girls. Rory and Dean meet each other in the Gilmore Girls pilot and they have a truly adorable scene. Rory makes a joke about Rosemary's Baby and is shocked and thrilled when Dean understands it. Pop culture references are a big part of the show for both Rory and Lorelai, so it's hard for them to bond with others who don't get their references.

After kissing at Doose's Market, which is awkward but cute, and Lorelai inviting Dean over for a movie night, Rory and Dean enter an official relationship. He's a sweet partner, making her a bracelet and always making time for her, and things seem to be going well, though their relationship isn't without a few TV stumbling blocks. When Rory learns that Dean likes the idea of a TV version of a 1950s housewife taking care of him, she decides to pretend to be one for the evening to teach him a lesson about gender stereotypes.

The two survive that stumbling block just fine. They also survive their entire relationship being put under a microscope in their small town. When Dean accompanies Rory to a dance at Chilton, the two end up leaving early after he gets into an argument with another student. They read for a while in the local dance studio, falling asleep, and spending the entire night out, only to be discovered by a bunch of gossiping adults the next morning.

Because Rory is so beloved by so many people in the small town of Star's Hollow, everyone always seems to have an opinion about her relationships in the show, even her first with Dean. While it's sweet that so many care about her, it's also a look at how gossip can get out of hand.

In fact, their relationship seems to be going well until he says he loves her on their three-month anniversary, and she can't say it back. While that's a common stumbling block in tv relationships, it briefly ends Rory and Dean's. Suddenly Rory is single and learning to wallow from her mom, who swears that junk food and pop culture will help her grieve the loss of Dean. Rory does briefly try to find comfort in Chilton student Tristan (Chad Michael Murray), but the series makes it clear that despite their chemistry, Rory and Tristan aren't right for one another.

Rory and Dean's relationship timeline makes for a mostly typical first love on television, especially in the first season as all of Rory's firsts are experienced with Dean. He's her first kiss, her first boyfriend, her first heartbreak, and the first person she tells she loves. All in just one season.

Rory sneaks into Doose's Market to get closer to Dean and Dean seeks Rory out at the wake for a cat named Cinnamon

Rory kisses Dean for the first time at Doose's Market and Lorelai invites Dean to spend their movie night with them as Dean and Rory officially become a couple

After a dance at Chilton, Dean and Rory accidentally stay out all night

Season 1, Episode 14 "That Damn Donna Reed"

Rory pretends to be a stereotypical 1950s housewife for an evening to teach Dean a lesson

Season 1, Episode 16 "Star-Crossed Lovers And Other Strangers"

Rory and Dean celebrate their 3-month anniversary and Dean tells her he loves her, but Rory doesn't say it back; Dean and Rory break up for the first time

Season 1, Episode 17 "The Breakup Part 2"

Rory kisses Tristan at a party while she and Dean are broken up

Season 1, Episode 20 "Love, Daisies, and Troubadours"

Rory tells Dean she loves him; Rory and Dean get back together the first time

In season 2 of Gilmore Girls, there are some problems in Dean and Rory's relationship, as he thinks she cares too much about Chilton and getting into Harvard. He complains about not getting enough time with her, which creates even more stress in her life. This is actually the crux of the problems for the couple throughout the Rory and Dean relationship timeline in the series. As much as the two care about one another, they are always moving in different directions, and that's made especially clear in season 2.

Rory thinks their relationship is flawless until Jess (Milo Ventimiglia) moves to town, and she starts to like him, too. Jess creates major waves for Dean and Rory, flirting with Rory every chance he gets. Fans can tell that this won't end well for Dean, and it seems like nothing can stop Jess and Rory's feelings for each other. While fans love Dean initially because Rory loves Dean, it's pretty clear that Jess and Rory might actually be a better match from their early meetings.

Even though Jess is hostile to everyone he meets, he's not to Rory, showing who he is underneath all the other drama he's dealing with, and that's what initially puts Dean on edge about him. Dean might be painted as insecure for much of season 2, but his fears about Jess being able to win Rory over are well-founded. Jess and Rory read the same books, share a lot of the same interests, and as Rory moves forward academically at Chilton, she moves farther away from Dean throughout season 2.

Rory takes Dean to dinner for the first time at her grandparents' house

Dean and Rory begin fighting about how much time she spends away from him

Season 2, Episode 5 "Nick & Nora/Sid& Nancy"

Rory meets Jess for the first time and realizes she's attracted to someone other than Dean

Season 2, Episode 9 "Run Away, Little Boy"

Dean gets jealous when Rory is cast as Juliet opposite Tristan as Romeo at Chilton

Dean and Jess get into a bidding war over Rory's picnic basket at a charity auction

Rory and Dean argue when he finds out Jess drops by when Lorelai is gone

Rory tutors Jess, but Jess takes the opportunity to make it a date, and they end up in a car accident

Season 2, Episode 22 "I Can't Get Started"

Rory and Jess kiss at Sookie's wedding and she starts to doubt her relationship with Dean

In season 3, the bumps in the road that are caused by Rory's connection with Jess are too much for Rory and Dean's relationship. It's not Rory, however, but Dean, who realizes that they can't keep trying to fix things. In fact, for the first six episodes of the season, Dean is rarely around and Rory finds herself constantly in Jess' orbit instead. Dean ends his relationship with Rory during the Stars Hollow dance marathon in the third season episode "They Shoot Gilmores, Don't They?" and while it's sad, it's not a huge surprise.

Rory and Jess start dating immediately and by the next episode, they're stealing kisses whenever they can, which must feel awkward for Dean since, of course, he still has to live in Stars Hollow. With Rory's later relationships in the series, he isn't there to witness her moving on. Rory, however, also has to witness Dean moving on as her relationship with Jess slowly fizzles out. It might seem like an end to the Rory and Dean timeline, but season 3 is still right in the middle of it.

Season 3, Episode 7 "They Shoot Gilmores, Don't They?"

Dean breaks up with Rory in front of everyone

Rory and Dean decide to try and be friends after everything that's happened

Rory sees Dean with his new girlfriend for the first time

Season 3, Episode 20 "Say Goodnight, Gracie"

Dean and Rory don't actually spend a lot of time around one another during season 4 of Gilmore Girls. Rory is busy trying to find her footing in college and Dean is navigating married life. They both, however, have difficulties in their personal lives and are eventually drawn back to one another. Rory has sex for the first time with Dean in the season 4 episode "Raincoats and Recipes." This changes their relationship, as after being exes for a while, they're now in a strange place where they realize they still share an attraction and real feelings.

When Lorelai comes home, Rory shares that she lost her virginity to Dean, and Lorelai can't understand why her daughter would make that choice because Dean is married to Lindsey (Arielle Kebbel). It's one of the rare times when the mother and daughter duo don't seem to see eye to eye, and it's also obvious that Rory and Dean have a bond that has never really gone away. When it comes to the Rory and Dean timeline, season 4 might not feature a lot of interactions between the two, but it does begin their journey to a more adult relationship.

Season 4, Episode 18 "Tick, Tick, Tick, Boom!"

Rory talks badly about Dean's new wife where others can hear her

Season 4, Episode 20 "Luke Can See Her Face"

Dean and Lindsey start having problems in their relationship

Season 4, Episode 22 "Raincoats and Recipes"

Rory sleeps with Dean, despite him still being married

Rory and Dean officially get back together in season 5 of Gilmore Girls after Lorelai's shock that Rory would sleep with someone who was married. This love story continues to be super rocky. When Lorelai suggests a double date with Luke and Dean, she and Rory struggle with how awkward it is, and Luke is upset because he doesn't approve of Dean. After attending a Friday Night Dinner, Dean feels left out and like he can't possibly be the right person for Rory now.

Season 5 finds the two of them falling back into old relationship patterns despite them no longer being teenagers. It's hard for Rory to balance being a Yale student and spending time with him, which echoes their high school days when he said she spent too much time studying. Dean works two jobs as well, so he's not available when Rory is. They break up again, and Rory starts seeing Logan (Matt Czuchry).

Season 5, Episode 1 "Say Goodbye to Daisy Miller"

Rory tries to find support from friends for her affair with Dean

Season 5, Episode 2 "A Messenger, Nothing More"

Rory writes her feelings for Dean out in a letter and Lindsey finds it

Season 5, Episode 4 "Tippecanoe and Taylor, Too"

Dean and Lindsey divorce, Dean and Rory get back together again

Season 5, Episode 5 "We Got Us A Pippi Virgin"

Lorelai tries to mend fences by inviting Dean to movie night with Luke and Rory

Rory's grandparents don't like the idea of her dating Dean and try to introduce her to better suitors; Dean breaks up with Rory for the last time

Season 5, Episode 18 "To Live And Let Diorama"

Dean's last appearance in the series; Dean warns Luke about holding Lorelai back from her dreams

In seasons 6 and 7 of Gilmore Girls, Rory is all about Logan, and their relationship leads to some negative changes in Rory's life. Rory and Dean seem to be out of each other's lives for good. In season 6, Rory sees Jess a few times, but no one hears what Dean is up to, as his last appearance is in the fifth season.

At first, it must seem like things are simpler with Logan since he's confident, fun-loving, and willing to commit to Rory, but of course, things get more complicated as Rory realizes Logan has some family problems that he tends to ignore. Logan's father Mitchum is very judgmental and demanding, and he doesn't support Rory and Logan's romance. There are no major milestones for Rory and Dean's relationship timeline in the final two seasons of Gilmore Girls though.

Rory still loves Stars Hollow as much as ever in the revival A Year In The Life, and she even bumps into Dean at Doose's Market, despite him having moved away with his new wife and children in the years in between the series and the revival. The meeting between the two exes provides closure for the characters about their relationship as much as it does the audience. It's the final spot on the Rory and Dean relationship timeline.

Doose's Market is a fitting place for them to see each other again since this was the setting of their first kiss and where Dean used to work when they began dating. The two haven't seen each other in years and only have a brief conversation. Rory thanks Dean for being so sweet to her and says that their relationship was a really good thing in her life. Dean is glad to hear that, but he has moved past Rory and Stars Hollow. He's married, has his own family, and doesn't seem to think about Rory anymore.

In a nice touch, Dean mentions that he's married to a woman named Ginny and has three kids as a nod to Padalecki's real-life relationship with actress Genevieve Padalecki (formerly Cortese). The two met while working together on Supernatural and have three children. Dean's time in the Gilmore Girls series is truly at an end, while Rory continues to figure out what she wants in her professional and romantic lives in Gilmore Girls: A Year In The Life.

Rory and Dean’s relationship timeline is more full than those of Rory and her other serious boyfriends throughout the show. While Rory has a flirtation with Tristan early in the series, her only serious relationships are with Dean, Jess, and Logan. Rory starts the series with Dean, but she ends the series with Logan, while she and Jess only have a few seasons together.

Logan might be who she ends the series with, but it’s Dean who has a more complete timeline when it comes to his relationship with Rory. Dean is who Rory experiences all of her firsts with. He isn’t just her first boyfriend, but her first love. In fact, Dean is the only boyfriend Rory repeatedly goes back to after years apart. She and Jess meet up after a long separation, but nothing happens between them other than Jess giving her advice. With Dean, Rory rekindles a romance, making their on-again-off-again relationship last over five years.

Dean and Rory also get a measure of closure, something that isn’t truly felt with Rory’s other relationships. She and Logan never feel finished since they have an affair in Gilmore Girls: A Year In The Life. Likewise, Rory and Jess have brief moments in one another’s lives as adults that feel as though their could be more to come from them. Dean’s relationship timeline with Rory is the only one that truly comes to an end in Gilmore Girls.

In the fictional town of Star's Hollow, single mother Lorelai Gilmore raises her high-achieving teenage daughter Rory. Mother and daughter rely on each other throughout their own life changes, romantic entanglements, and friendships.

Gilmore Girls: A Year in the Life revisits the beloved characters of Stars Hollow nearly a decade since the original series' conclusion. This revival chronicles four seasons of life changes, focusing on evolving relationships, family dynamics, and personal development, set against the familiar small-town backdrop.

The 9 Best Pop-Up Canopy Tents for Outdoor Activities and Events

📅 Apr 06, 2021  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Insta-shade and rain coverage for all your backyard barbecues, park outings, and tailgates.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Whether it's a weekend barbecue, a tailgate, or the sidelines of a kid’s soccer game, pop-up canopy tents are the best solution for substantial shade and shelter at outdoor events.

I’ve personally researched, tested, and essentially lived under pop-up canopies over the last few years, doing activities like puzzles and watching movies, and working in what I love to call my second office. If there's one thing I've learned, it's that while pop-up canopy tents may look similar, they’re actually purpose-built, and the details that distinguish one from the next aren’t always obvious to the casual observer.

For example, a tent that's perfect for a tailgate party is lightweight, super fast to set up, and has a relatively compact footprint. The canopy that’s in my backyard took two people to set up and is a larger, more durable structure. A pop-up canopy for the beach is designed to stay in place when a gust of wind rushes on shore.

Below, I walk you through the best pop-up canopy tents for specific activities and include info about why I love it and where it falls short. Following my reviews, I’ve laid out key considerations to take into account when shopping for a pop-up canopy.

This pop-up canopy takes our top spot because of its durable water-resistant canopy, height-adjustable frame, and top-point air ventilation.

The canopy's 300-denier polyester fabric has a water-resistant polyurethane lining and is coated in silver, which helps protect you from UV rays. It’s also rated UPF 50+, so it withstands hot, sunny days and improves the tent’s longevity.

The powder-coated steel frame is height-adjustable and very easy to open, thanks to large push-button brackets. We also appreciate that the canopy is finished at the edges with white trim and that its corners extend midway down the side poles. Plus, the top air vent allows for air and smoke from below to escape instead of accumulate, so you can easily place a controlled campfire underneath it on a rainy day. Similarly, the vent lets wind blow by more easily without toppling over.

The tent comes in a rainbow of colors and three sizes. Stakes, ropes, sandbags, and a wheeled carrying case that doubles as a backpack are all included. The sandbags provided may be too light to hold the canopy down by themselves in heavy winds, so you may need to invest in larger ones.

For something with a little more coverage and durability, we recommend going with a pop-up canopy tent that has sidewalls. We especially like this model from Eurmax because the sidewalls attach with velcro, so they can easily come on or off depending on weather conditions.

The strong, cross-trussed frame has a powder-coated finish and adjusts to three heights, plus the tent is topped with a heavy-duty polyester canopy, which is water-resistant and rated UPF 50+.

The sidewalls are a lighter polyester, and seams throughout are sealed to prevent leaks. Along with the tent, you’ll also get four sandbags to ensure stability. We also appreciate that this comes with a wheeled carrying case too, because this tent weighs just over 60 pounds.

If you’re looking for a budget-friendly pop-up canopy that doesn’t skimp on durability, this model from Phi Villa is tough to beat. In addition to its durability, one-person setup, and three height settings, it comes with something I have never seen included with even high-end canopies: an attachable, hanging, five-pocket organizer that holds essentials like keys, snacks, books, or toys.

The canopy itself is made from durable, flame-retardant Oxford fabric, which is especially useful if you plan to grill under the canopy or nearby. It also comes with multiple ways to keep it grounded in wind and storms, including stakes, tie-down ropes, and sandbags. With purchase, you also get a one-year limited warranty for the frame and six months for fabrics and accessories.

My only gripe with this pop-up canopy is the grommeted vent holes along the sides that allow rain to drip through. If you plan to set it up on a rainy day, I highly recommend finding a way to plug each hole to block the rain from pouring through and soaking you and your things.

If it’s fast, easy shade you need and on a budget, the Ozark Trail slant-leg canopy is worth a look. It’s not as durable as the Eurmax or ABC Canopy options above, but at under $100, it’s a solid choice that delivers exactly what you need: a steel frame, a polyester canopy top, and a snap-button setup that takes one person and just a few minutes to get off the ground.

One thing to note related to the slant-leg construction is that while the dimensions are advertised as 10x10, the actual shaded footprint is closer to 8x8 feet (64 square feet). Still, for casual use on a day where weather is fair and free of rain, like a kid's birthday party at the park or a weekend camping trip, it's a genuinely good value.

For tailgating, you need a canopy that is strong, easy to set up, and doesn’t need to be staked to the ground. The Crown Shade pop-up canopy delivers, and as an added bonus there are loads of colors to choose from.

Its patented locking system uses a central hub that locks all four legs into place with a single push, which means it’s ideal for one-person assembly. I also appreciate the sidewall included for protection from the elements, and to create a more intimate space when lined up in the parking lot with other fans and their canopies.

The 150-denier silver-coated fabric is lighter than some of the heavier-duty options on this list; it does make for a lighter load when lugging to games. Make sure you throw a couple of bricks or rocks in the car to fill the included sandbags for extra protection from gusts of wind.

When it comes to finding a great camping canopy, you need something extra durable that can withstand not only the elements but also being lugged to the campsite with ease, or able to be pitched safely on uneven ground. If you’re looking for something rugged that’s both functional and dependable, this pop-up canopy from Coleman is the best choice.

The Coleman Oasis has a center-hub opening and closing system. The frame pops open with a single push and takedown is just as easy thanks to the pull-strap. The steel frame is built to withstand winds up to 35 mph (when properly staked with the included ropes).

The UPF 50+ fabric handles sun exposure well while the vented canopy lets heat escape. The only gripe I have is that it does not come with sandbags, so if you are expecting bad weather, you may want to pack your own sandbag set up. This canopy comes in two sizes 10 x10 or 13x13, and you can opt for a one side wall design for extra protection from the elements.

Pop-up canopy tents for constant backyard use need to look good, be durable and spacious, and keep bugs out during dinner. The Crown Shades 11x14 gazebo fits the bill on all these points.

It’s roomy enough for a full-length patio table and has strong zip-close mesh screen walls. The pre-assembled, rope-strung netting is far easier to install than traditional hook-by-hook netting I’ve encountered on other models.

The reinforced steel frame with an eight-point center locking system feels more substantial underfoot than most. Also, the double-roof vented design keeps the space noticeably cooler during humid backyard dinners compared to single-layer canopy tops I've used.

While I have set this up by myself, it’s easier and safer as a two-person job. Keep in mind that when the walls are zipped closed, the actual space is 9 by 12 feet, not 11 x 14; the lost ground space is due to the canopy design overhang.

Worrying about a traditional pop-up canopy tent getting swept up on a windy beach day ruins the vibe. That is why I am sending a huge shoutout to the Shibumi Shade. This thing was made for seriously windy beach days.

In fact, it needs just a little wind to work well, which the beach is never short of. Instead of a rigid frame, it uses an arched, wind-powered flowing design that catches the ocean breeze and holds its shape with as little as 3 mph of wind. It weighs under five pounds, fits in a small bag and pitches in about three minutes.

It covers about 150 square feet, enough shade for a group of up to eight people, and the open-air design keeps airflow moving at all times, so I can actually stay cool and protected from the sun all day long.

For the overall and value picks, I looked at frames and fabrics that could reasonably handle every activity on this list. I prioritized peak height, fabric construction and durability, and included accessories like sandbags and tie-down ropes and stakes.

For the budget pick, I looked for a canopy that delivered a stable, easy setup even with less durable materials. I also made notes where the actual shade coverage falls short of the advertised footprint—an important thing to note if you require specific square footage.

For tailgating, I focused on setup speed and the ability to stay in place in a parking lot. For the backyard, I prioritized walls for bug protection, ventilation, and a larger footprint suited to dining or large seating furniture rather than simple folding chairs and plastic tables. For camping, I weighed wind resistance against portability.

And for the beach, I intentionally looked outside the traditional steel-frame category, prioritizing a design that works well with wind and sand, and, of course, ease of transport across dunes and sand.

The two things that determine how long a canopy survives in the elements are the frame material and the fabric denier (a measure of fabric thickness).

I always look for powder-coated steel frames on anything I plan to use regularly, since they resist rust and hold up to repeated ups and downs better than lighter alloy frames. For fabric, anything rated CPAI-84 has passed a flame-resistance standard, and higher denier counts (ideally 300D and up) hold up to UV exposure and light rain without degrading as quickly.

If I'm buying a canopy that'll live outdoors semi-permanently, like my beloved backyard gazebo I write about above, I prioritize a double-roof or vented design, since trapped heat and pooled rainwater are the two things most likely to shorten a canopy's lifespan. It’s worth the extra spend.

Nearly every canopy on this list offers multiple height settings, and I've come to see this as non-negotiable rather than nice-to-have. It can dramatically help in wind and rain: lower settings help in windy conditions by reducing how much the canopy catches the breeze, while higher settings give more headroom and better airflow underneath. I also pay attention to whether sandbags and tie-down ropes are included, since staking out a canopy with ropes at an angle also dramatically improves its wind resistance compared to relying on the legs and sandbags alone.

This is where the mechanism matters as much as the material. Central-hub or "one-push" designs, like the ones on the Crown Shades and Coleman Oasis, let me pop the whole frame solo from the middle rather than pulling each corner out individually, which is both faster and easier for one person. I also look at how the canopy packs away: a bag with a wheeled base and a dedicated pocket for stakes and ropes comes in handy when dragging to and from home.

How much a canopy weighs, and how well it travels, matters differently depending on where I'm taking it and what I need it for. For a canopy that's mostly staying in the backyard, weight barely registers; I’ll take a heavy-duty frame and canopy that weighs more than I can comfortably carry happily. However, for tailgating or camping, where I'm loading and unloading a vehicle full of stuff, I care a lot about whether the carry bag has wheels versus just a shoulder strap that weighs me down. And for the beach, where I'm hauling gear across soft sand, weight becomes the single most important factor, which is exactly why a 4-pound wind-powered shade like the Shibumi Shade beats a 40-pound steel frame every time.

In terms of construction, there are two different types of frames: pyramid (sometimes referred to as “cathedral”) and cross-trussed. Pyramid frames are generally less expensive and offer more headroom by design. The hollow pyramid structure keeps the top space up to the point of the covering. The biggest drawback with pyramid canopy tents is they are less sturdy and more subject to breaking or bending under heavy weight. This is because the poles that hold the point up top are single poles, angled, and not cross-trussed, which means they may bow or bend under pressure.

Your alternative is a canopy tent with cross trusses across the center and one pole that rises to create the same pointed top. This is, as you might have guessed, the cross-trussed model. These have a lower ceiling than pyramid options, but they make up for that with a much sturdier construction. The added cross trusses across the canopy make them more resistant to bending or breaking, though it also makes them more expensive and heavier to carry.

Depending on use, you may want to consider a pop-up canopy tent with sidewalls or a model with add-on walls sold separately to give yourself extra customization. Sidewalls provide some protection from wind, and if you're using a tent at a farmers’ market or fair, they can make your pop-up more inviting. Similarly, even vented sidewalls, which let air pass through, can offer some protection from bees and mosquitoes.

Most pop-up canopy tents come with extras like stakes, sandbags, and tie-down ropes for keeping the tent secure, especially on windy days. They also usually include a wheeled storage bag, along with an optional carrying strap for easy portability.

If you choose a tent that does not come with these items, you may want to consider purchasing them separately. For instance, these sandbags will keep any of our tents in place when stakes aren’t an option and the wind is high. They’re also designed to last with sun protection and thick, waterproof material.

Most pop-up canopy tents are designed for simple setup and can often be assembled by a single person with average strength. Taking the canopy down is typically just as straightforward. That said, it’s important to follow the instructions carefully. The canopy fabric usually needs to be properly positioned and secured on the frame before fully extending the structure. Skipping or rushing this step puts unnecessary strain on the material and may lead to stretching or tearing.

For the most part, pop-up canopies are designed to handle wind gusts up to about 15 mph with ease. That said, I have learned that properly securing your canopy is one of the most important factors in determining if your canopy can withstand higher wind gusts or major storms. Properly secure your canopy to its frame according to the manufacturer's instructions, and place the stakes in the feet of the canopy when possible. I also add sandbags for extra security from high winds. (I actually fill mine with bricks or heavy rocks because who has 50 pounds of sand lying around?) By properly securing the canopy and the frame, you will minimize the danger of the frame shifting and moving, which can tear the canopy.

To date, I haven’t encountered a pop-up canopy that didn’t include some form of anchoring system. These anchor systems typically come in a few different styles, such as stakes that fit through holes drilled into the canopy feet, grommeted loops at each corner for securing ropes or heavy-duty cord to ground spikes, or weighted sandbags that attach around each leg.

Whatever is provided, it’s essential that you use it. That said, if you choose to leave your canopy up for an extended period of time or all year long and a major storm is going to blow through, use your judgment as to whether you should take it down completely or reinforce the anchor system.

The 5 Best OLED TVs You Can Buy Right Now

The 8 Best Slim Wallets to Keep Necessities Secure

Husqvarna 460 Review: Big Value in a Big Chainsaw
