The 7 Best Smokeless Fire Pits We Tested (and Yes, They Produced Hardly Any Smoke)

📅 Mar 16, 2021  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From set-it-and-forget-it backyard centerpieces to grab-and-go options for weekend camping trips, we tried well-regarded options from Solo Stove, Breeo, Blue Sky, and more.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Engineered to contain flames and burn fuel more completely, smokeless fire pits deliver ambient warmth with a lot less smoke in your eyes than a traditional campfire. They achieve this through two points of combustion: The first happens at the bottom, where airflow feeds burning wood; the second occurs at the top, where the fire’s smoke and gas output are burned off. And while no smokeless fire pit is 100 percent smokeless, if you use best practices (like burning dry wood) they come pretty darn close.

We tested smokeless fire pits in our New York and Pennsylvania backyards, building fires rather than judging them on specs alone. Along with smoke output, we considered size, which affects wood capacity and where the heat actually lands, as well as how easy each pit was to empty of ash and store once done, among other factors. We also used them repeatedly over time to see how their finishes held up, because a brand-new pit can look very different after several fires.

Solo Stove is the brand most people associate with smokeless fire pits (though Breeo was the first game in town). The latest joiner in Solo Stove’s lineup is the Summit, an upgraded take on the ever-popular Bonfire. Its 23-pound weight makes it just as easy to lift and store off-season, and there’s even less setup required. All-in-all, it’s the best smokeless fire pit for most people who want to enjoy a backyard fire pit that can be stored off-season.

Because the Summit is four to five inches shorter than the Bonfire, it does a better job of keeping your feet warm, one of the major gripes our Test Editor Brad Ford had of the earlier model. He says that while it holds less firewood, it undoubtedly produces a higher flame that extends well above the rim.

Another improvement he points out is the Summit’s integrated stand, which he says is sturdier and allows for better airflow than that of the Bonfire’s detached stand. There’s also a deeper ash pan, which is particularly helpful because the Summit burns through wood fast and needs frequent reloading. In terms of price, the 19.5-inch Summit is $100 more than a Bonfire of the same size, an extra cost that’s well worth the investment.

Full Solo Stove Summit Smokeless Fire Pit Review

After trying out the Breeo X Series 24 for several months, test editor Brad Ford loved it. According to Ford, “One of the coolest details of the X Series 24 is its Corten finish. (It is also available in a stainless-steel finish.) Corten is a structural steel alloy you often see used for bridges or power line towers. It’s designed to oxidize, or rust, creating a natural finish that protects the steel rather than continuing to degrade it. This eliminates the frustration I’ve had with some stainless-steel fire pits that discolor after a couple uses.”

Beyond this, Ford also liked, “that Breeo intended for their smokeless fire pits to be used for cooking from the get-go, which means they’ve got accessories for the X 24 that work very well. And most of their cooking accessories are compatible with all their fire pits. Top this all off with the fact that the X 24 is made right in Lancaster PA, USA, unlike most other popular brands.”

The Breeo X 24 weighs more than twice as much as the other models we’ve tested, so it’s not exactly portable—but it is long-lasting.

As the largest pick of the lot, the X Series was easy to load with wood and start a fire. We fit 18- to 20-inch logs into it, laying them across the raised X in the bottom of the pit. This patented design keeps the logs high, allowing air to flow in and feed the fire from the bottom.

Cleanup required scooping ash out, as the unit is too heavy to dump out easily—Breeo sells a shovel designed to ease this process. The brand also offers several accessories for cooking over the fire, which make the X Series as much a grill as it is a fire pit.

This hefty, all-black fire pit is a great-value addition to an outdoor setup. Its wide diameter gives you plenty of room to build a substantial fire, and the shape and airflow make for a good, largely smokeless burn. It takes both wood and pellets right out of the box, no adapter or switch-up required. And the lift-out ash catch has two handles, making it one of the easiest models to empty. (You still have to reach into the drum to lift it out, though, so wear gloves.)

The black coating is practical, too: Unlike stainless steel, it doesn’t show every fire mark. Once in place, it blends nicely with patio furniture, particularly setups with black metal legs. While it does scratch more easily than others we tested, you can touch it up with high-heat spray paint, just like you would a grill.

Some things to bear in mind: At 35 pounds, it’s heavier than many of the models we tested, so you’re unlikely to move it around much once it’s situated. The price doesn’t include a cover, which you’ll definitely want if it’s going to live outside for the season.

Because the ash catch sits inside the main body, with no separate base beneath it, we’d also place this one on a fireproof mat to be extra safe. And this may be petty, but the branding is the most prominent of any pit we tested: you’ll be looking at the words “Blue Sky” on the base every time you sit down.

If you want to bring the campfire with you wherever you go, the Packfire portable fire pit is an ideal choice. It packs down into a backpack, so you can carry it on your shoulders and set it up wherever your adventures take you. In fact, its innovative design won it a spot in our 2026 Home Awards.

The fire pit is composed of three sections: the base, which serves as a stand and ash pan; the body, which, when fully set up, surrounds the base; and the ring, which holds the body in place. Cleanup is easy: Simply lift the ring and body off the base, pour the ashes out, and break everything back down for travel.

It’s worth noting that even though it’s built for travel, the fire pit is still a bit cumbersome to carry around. While our Director of Product Content, Will Egensteiner was testing the Packfire, he said its 27.5 weight was a factor. “As I heaved it onto my back to put it away, I almost tipped over. And I’m not a small guy.”

The substance of this fire pit starts with the box: One of the largest and heaviest we had to maneuver into location for testing (only the Breeo was heavier), moving it really requires two people or a dolly. Some of that initial box weight comes from the generous bag of wood pellets packed inside, but the pit itself, at 45 pounds with nothing in it, is hardly lightweight.

Assembly is a little less impressive. Attaching the legs required a drill, as, while an Allen key and screws were included, some of the holes weren’t perfectly aligned, making the process more fiddly than it should have been. It does, however, come with spare screws.

Once assembled, though, this thing is a very good-looking workhorse. The large bag of wood pellets kept going for about 35 minutes before burning down to embers; produced virtually no smoke; and the heat output was surprisingly high.

Simply by flipping the inside container, you can also fire up 14-inch logs inside. The exterior frame stayed remarkably cool and, given it’s black, didn’t discolor the way the stainless steel ones did. While the inside picked up some black streaks from the flames, they looked more like evidence of a real fire than damage, which we actually liked.

The ash disposal is a true highlight. You don’t have to lift, twist, or tip the pit to empty it: An ash pan slides out from the side, shovel-style, so you can dump the refuse without moving the fire pit at all. It also comes with a perfectly fitted cover, which is particularly useful given how difficult this one would be to haul away when not in use.

This seriously smart, compact fire pit is made for small spaces and taking on the road. It’s lightweight, straightforward to assemble, and comes with six pieces: a pit and ash can that sit on top of one another; two pieces that twist together to form a fire poker (a little tight to sync up, but ultimately quite a useful piece); and a spark screen with a screw-in knob to lift it (also a very nice extra).

Truly, the whole kit is quite remarkable, given the price. The ash catch is also cleverly designed to fit inside the fire pit for storage, so there’s nothing extra to haul around when not in use. The black finish looks sharp and has the added benefit of hiding burn marks better than lighter finishes. (The downside is, it scratches easily.)

The whole thing packs into a carrying bag with a shoulder strap and drawstring closure. It’s also lighter than any other model we tested, making it an excellent choice for beach parties, camping trips, or anywhere you need to carry your fire pit more than a few feet.

There is one important caveat: Because the pit is so compact, you’ll mostly be limited to twigs, which burn quickly. Use wood pellets instead, for a longer, more consistent fire. The carrying bag also feels flimsy, like it could break down quickly with repeat use, but, for the price, that may not matter.

This one looks like a saucer and comes with just three pieces: a main body that locks securely into the ash holder base and a wind guard that twists onto the rim. It’s also one of the roomier pits we tested, with a wider, more-open design that accommodates both more and larger pieces of wood than many others. The manufacturer says it handles logs up to one pound. We’d have preferred a measurement in inches, but went with 16 inches, which it handled better than the Tiki. You can also add a griddle attachment to turn it into a cooker.

This one really does produce a roaring fire that can warm four people comfortably. The wide, funnel-like shape seems to encourage a bigger, more expansive flame; sitting around it felt more like a bonfire than simply burning wood in a container. After 25 minutes, we still had a substantial fire going. Its lower stature kept our feet warm too.

The biggest downside is ash removal. The pan in the base is relatively shallow, and the wide fire pit is awkward to twist and lift off for cleaning. You’ll want long arms—or a second person—to do it comfortably. The stainless-steel finish also turned a light bronze after just one use. A cover costs extra.

Before lighting a single fire, we evaluated the things that can make or break a smokeless fire pit before you ever use them. We assessed the packaging and how well each pit was protected in transit, weighed every model, and evaluated assembly. We read the instructions, noting whether they were clear, complete, and actually useful.

We considered whether it could be disassembled for storage, and what came in the box versus what required an additional purchase. While We had plenty of outdoor space to work with, safety always came first: we set every pit on heavy-duty pavers and kept a fire extinguisher close at hand.

Then we lit them up. We used comparable loads of seasoned firewood to assess how easily each pit started, how much smoke it produced, how evenly it burned, and how much heat it put out. We paid attention to flame height and consistency, too, and whether the fire actually warmed people sitting around it—not just looked good from a distance. We measured logs to see exactly what each pit could accommodate and tested wood pellets where models supported them.

Finally, we evaluated the less glamorous but equally important details: how easy each pit was to tend, how much ash accumulated, how easily that ash could be removed, and what it took to clean and put each model away. And because a fire pit can be beautiful at 7 p.m. and a pain in the ass at 10 p.m., we tested for both.

Whether you want a smokeless fire pit to heat your outdoor space or be more of an ambient seasonal moment is an important consideration. For long nights by the fire with family and friends, you’ll likely want to invest in a larger model that measures 24 inches in diameter. In testing, models that size and up exuded warmth for several feet, and could easily warm four to six people.

They could also handle 14- to 16-inch-long logs. The trade-off: Models this size are a lot heavier and harder to move. If you’re looking for more of a warming accent, narrower fire pits work wonders. You can also maximize some models’ output by adding a heat deflector that sits above the flames and pushes heat out laterally, instead of letting it escape upward.

Ideally, the smokeless fire pit should work right out of the box, with limited-to-no setup, besides setting the pit on a base. It should also break down easily. If you’ll be storing it between fires or seasons, a model that’s easy to disassemble, carry, and put away is key.

The shallower and wider the smokeless fire pit, the easier it is to make a fire from scratch. You simply have more base room to work with to set up your paper, kindling, and wood, and less depth to maneuver to light your fire once ready. No matter your choice, you’ll definitely want a lighter with a long metal wand and a safety catch. None of the models we tested included one.

Burning pellets instead of wood was the closest we got to a truly smokeless fire during our testing. Made of compressed wood waste like sawdust, chips, and shavings, pellets are easy to light, always bone dry, and produce a hotter, more consistent flame than logs. There are trade-offs, though: They lack the classic crackle and pop of a wood-burning fire and that wonderful smell.

Bags of the stuff also typically cost more than a handful of logs at the hardware store, yet burn about the same length of time. But, if you’re allergic to smoke, don’t want your sweaters to smell of it, have wet wood drying out, or want a reliable and even fire every single time, pellets are an excellent way to go. Some fire pits work with them right out of the box; others need an adapter.

Every smokeless pit burns wood. Many smokeless fire pits can also use wood pellets right out of the box. Some, like Solo Stove, need an adapter, so the tiny pellets don’t drop right through the pit’s air holes into the ashcan below. Some portable options may only be wide or deep enough to handle twigs (pellets may be a better option for those).

Pro tip: Wet or green wood will always be slow to burn and emit a lot of smoke; use a moisture meter to suss out whether your logs are dry enough to light up before putting a match to them.

If you plan to use your fire pit regularly, easy ash removal should be a deciding factor. The last thing you want to have to do is pick up and tip a heavy steel drum over a trashcan in order to empty it of ash. Instead, look for a removable ash pan or tray that allows you to lift the ash out cleanly once the pit has fully cooled (usually 12 to 24 hours after a fire has ended). Otherwise, you’ll need a shovel to scoop out the ash.

If you don’t have space to leave your fire pit outside all year, think about who will move it and how often. Even a small pit designed for camping trips can weigh as much as 20 pounds. Fortunately, these options usually come with a carrying bag, so you can sling them over your shoulder. Meanwhile, larger models can top 45 pounds (the 24-inch Breeo weighs just over 60 pounds), which matters when you have to move it under cover or into the garage at the end of the season. Units that come apart make this easier; having a dolly or hand truck on hand helps, too.

Material affects how long your new pit will look the way it does on arrival. While stainless steel looks shiny and modern from the start, heat from the flames will discolor it, and potentially quite quickly, as some testing showed. (You can restore its original shine with a stainless-steel cleaner such as Bar Keepers Friend, but that’s extra work.) Corten-steel usually costs more but forms a protective rust-colored patina that looks attractively weathered over time. Painted or powder-coated steel usually costs less, but can scratch and chip with repeat use.

Whatever finish you choose, keeping your smokeless fire pit empty of ash and covered when it’s not in use will help extend its life. Rainwater sitting in a fire pit full of ash can create a corrosive, alkaline slurry that accelerates rust and can damage the metal. Some models come with a cover as part of their bundle; others charge extra for it. Our reviews note whether a cover is included in every pick.

Smokeless fire pits can be a lot more than a place to warm your hands on chilly nights. In some cases, you can turn them into an outdoor kitchen. Brands like Breeo, Solo Stove, and Cuisinart have grill grates, griddles, pizza ovens, or roasting sticks that let you cook over an open fire. Other add-ons also make a difference, like a cover, if you plan to leave it outside when not in use; a spark screen, particularly if you have toddlers and pets around (though, warning: they can also get hot to the touch); and fire pokers or ashtray lifters, so you don’t get your hands dirty. Definitely check the accessory lineup before you buy your pit; they’re often model-specific. A pair of fire-resistant gloves is always a good investment.

While product photos often depict smokeless fire pits sitting on wood decks or backyards, it’s worth noting that their bases can get hot enough to scorch or discolor lawns, wood decks, composite, and asphalt. Ideally, yours should sit on brick, concrete, stone, or sand. But in the absence of that, a dedicated base (sometimes included, other times sold separately) helps raise the unit off the ground. For an even safer setup, consider adding a fireproof mat underneath. You should also keep a bucket of water or a fire extinguisher nearby and check for local burn bans before lighting. For more on fire pit safety, check out our story.

Arabella Bowen is a seasoned product tester who specializes in travel, design, tech, home improvement, and outdoor essentials. She’s reviewed everything from roller bags and cameras to hiking equipment and home decor for outlets such as The Montreal Gazette, Fodor's, AFAR, and US Park Pass. Arabella can often be found immersed in DIY projects at her mid-century modern house in upstate New York—a hands-on pursuit that inspires many trips to Lowe’s.

All 11 Titan Types In Attack On Titan Explained

📅 Mar 07, 2021  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

In Attack on Titan, the Titans are much more than oversized monsters lurking beyond humanity's walls. What initially appears to be a terrifying fight for survival gradually develops into a complicated history involving ancient powers, political conflicts, and the origins of the creatures themselves. As Eren Yeager and his fellow soldiers uncover more about their enemies, the true variety of Titan abilities becomes increasingly difficult to ignore.

From seemingly mindless Pure Titans to the extraordinary powers possessed by the Nine Titan Shifters, each form serves a different purpose and presents its own threats. Some rely on overwhelming strength and numbers, while others have specialized abilities capable of changing the outcome of an entire war. Understanding these distinctions is essential to appreciating the series' lore and worldbuilding, so here is a breakdown of all 11 major Titan types featured in Attack on Titan.

Pure Titans resemble humans but with varying levels of enlarged forms. The majority of the observed Titans, both in the manga and the anime, belong to this category, their overarching feature being their inability to form and process thoughts.

Despite being made similarly to other Titan types, Pure Titans are mindless and tend to attack without discrimination. They are dangerous due to their sheer numbers, formidable strength, and enormous size. Pure Titans have been used as weapons of war, first by Eldia and then by Marley, and can only be killed by slashing the nape of their neck.

A notable example of a Pure Titan would be the humanoid dubbed Bean, who first appeared when the gates of Trost District were broken by Attack on Titan's epic Colossal Titan.

Bean was one of the two Titans kept alive for the sake of experimentation in an attempt to better understand the nature of these beasts and their innate weaknesses.

The Abnormal Titans are one of the most bizarre Titan types. Abnormals are Pure Titans that display unusual behavior and intelligence, allowing them to perform unpredictable moves. They often tend to ignore nearby humans and move on to strategic locations to inflict more damage.

Abnormals are adept at performing tremendous leaps, running, speed-walking, and in a few cases, communicating in human tongues. An Abnormal Titan’s ability to form thoughts and speech has been chronicled in the diary of Ilse Langnar, who had run into a hesitant Titan amid an expedition massacre.

After Hange Zoë and Levi’s squad find the diary, it is revealed that the Titan addressed Ilse as “Lady Ymir”, mistaking her for Attack on Titan's Founder, Ymir Fritz.

Confused by the possibility that a Titan can experience emotions, Ilse became increasingly frustrated during her attempts at communication, ending with the Titan crushing her skull whilst shedding tears. The existence of the Abnormal Titans forces humans to approach the beasts with a more nuanced perspective. This revelation proved to the remaining soldiers that not all Titans are mindless killers.

As the name suggests, the Attack Titan is an offensive powerhouse, standing at 15 meters tall and characterized by a highly muscular build and flesh-less jaws.

Eren Yaeger would later improve the Attack Titan’s metrics by drinking the Armor serum that allowed him to harden the Titan’s skin during pivotal moments.

As his default Titan Form, Eren was able to receive memories from those who possessed the Attack Titan and those who had yet to inherit it. This would later come to help him manipulate his father, Grisha, to cause his own Titan creation subsequently.

Although these memories allow the user to gain insights, they are often incomplete and short snippets.

Reiner Braun’s Armored Titan stands at 15 meters and was initially inspired to share a ​​​​​resemblance to WWE Superstar Brock Lesnar.

The Armored Titan has thick, hardened plates all over its body, acting as a natural defense against external attacks, including cannon hits, steel blades, and vertical maneuvering equipment, making it the most defensively strong of the Titan types.

In addition to this, these Attack on Titan Titans are able to harden their arms and feet, creating sharp claws in the process. The Armored Titan's only weakness seems to be the areas on the back of its exposed joints.

The armor is not capable of withstanding high-power weaponry, such as the Mid-East Allied Forces’ artillery.

Users of the largest Titan type, the Colossal Titan, can control the energy produced during their transformation to create various effects, such as when Bertolt attacks the Trost District, and his transformation triggers a powerful wind blast.

The Colossal Titan can also create a mushroom cloud of dust and rubble, leading to the death of many Survey Corps members during the Battle of Shiganshina District. As indicated by its name, the Colossal Titan is known for its enormous size, able to peer over the edge of Wall Mari and powerful enough to take down the outer gate with a single kick.

A collaboration between MAPPA and Hot Pepper Beauty reimagines the most iconic characters of Attack on Titan in a way that has never been seen before.

They possess a unique ability to control the steam emitted from their bodies, burning and repelling attacks in proximity. During his final moments in a fight against Armin and Mikasa, Eren's Titan form engages in a hand-to-hand fight with the former in his Colossal Titan form.

While Eren bests Armin in terms of physical and strategic prowess, Armin manages to immobilize him long enough for Mikasa to enter his Titan's mouth and kill him before kissing him goodbye.

Much like other powerful Titans, the Female Titan exercises a certain degree of influence over Pure Titans through a scream-based ability. However, this attraction ability has its disadvantages: once arriving near the Female Titan, the others tend to attack and eat its body.

The Female Titan is known for its tremendous endurance and mobility, able to harden its skin at will in order to shatter blades of ultra-hard steel upon contact.

Interestingly, Annie can also come out of her Titan’s nape to create a hardened crystal cocoon around herself for protective purposes.

During the 57th Exterior Scouting Mission Arc, Mikasa succeeds in slicing the Female Titan's fingers off, allowing Eren to rip the Titan form apart. However, after winning the fight, he began to merge with the Titan and required Levi’s help to remove him and stop the process.

The Beast Titan is slightly larger than most and exhibits animal traits that depend upon the user. They are known to have displayed traits common in elk, birds, bulls, and wolves, among others, as shown in Attack on Titan's Season 2 opening, while Zeke’s Titan assumed an ape-like form.

An interesting note in the series indicates that because Zeke’s spinal fluid was used to transform Falco into a Titan, it later gave his Jaw Titan falcon-like epithets similar to those of the Beast Titan.

Moreover, Zeke’s Beast Titan possessed the formidable ability to throw objects with great accuracy and destructive capability, notably during the battle of Shiganshina District, wherein most of the Survey Corps were wiped out by crushed rocks.

Due to his royal blood, Zeke’s Beast Titan holds the ability to turn Subjects of Ymir into Pure Titans.

Possessing greater endurance levels than Pure Titans and other members of the Nine Titans, the Cart Titan can partake in longer missions without having to pause for rest.

This Titan’s absorption resistance allows it to maintain its beastly form and wear armaments such as battle armor, mobile machine gun platforms, and cargo packs. As opposed to most bipedal Titans, the Cart Titan assumes a quadrupedal form, which potentially hinders the user from walking upright in human form.

In terms of weaknesses, the Cart Titan has lower defenses and slower regenerative abilities as opposed to the others, and is more likely to be taken down by a fatal blow.

However, similar to the Jaw Titan, the Cart Titan possesses immense speed, such as when it defeated Zeke and Reiner during the Battle of Shiganshina. This shocking characteristic even managed to catch Attack on Titan's strongest soldier, Levi, off guard.

As indicated by its name, the Jaw Titan possesses a powerful set of jaws that can tear through almost anything, even if it is considered to be the weakest of Attack on Titan's Titan Shifters.

They can move fast enough to catch some of the Nine off-guard and cause severe damage to armored opponents. Those who hold the power of the Jaw Titan possess incredible speed and agility.

However, the jaw manifests in slightly varying forms with respect to different users, such as Ymir only having hardened teeth, while Falco and Marcel had hardened, armored masks and jaw plating that were almost bulletproof.

Attack on Titan's War Hammer Titan has powers that allow it to produce and manipulate structures made of hardened Titan flesh, weaponizing them in the form of spikes and blades with serrated edges.

Theo Magath states that while a formidable asset, the War Hammer Titan’s structural hardening should be used in moderation, as constant usage can lead to serious exhaustion.

However, unlike the rest of the Nine, the War Hammer Titan can remove its control center from its nape, connecting the Titan and the host with a long cable of flesh, a unique ability that the rest of the Nine in Attack on Titan do not possess.

We Tested Home Generators to Find the Best Picks for a Power Outage

📅 Feb 15, 2021  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

By Glenda Taylor, Austin Fracchia, Mark Wolfe, Zach Lazzari

Updated Nov 3, 2026 9:44 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

During a power outage, a home generator can keep the lights on, the HVAC running, and the appliances powered, but there are a lot of options out there, from whole-house to quiet inverter models. Because we’re big on helping our readers navigate the array of products that make life easier, our team tackled the task of testing home generators.

These independent power sources range in output for a variety of needs. After researching more than 40 top models, we selected the highest-scoring models in a range of categories for hands-on testing. Our favorite was the Champion 7500-Watt Dual-Fuel Generator thanks to its versatile operation and multiple outlets.

Whether you’re looking for a generator to run an entire home or to power only a few essentials, you can find a top-notch one in this lineup. Keep reading to find out how each one performed and to learn more about what to consider when shopping for the best home generator for your needs.

Bottom line: Our comprehensive testing reveals top home generators for every need, from budget-friendly to whole-house solutions.

When shopping for a generator, it’s easy to become overwhelmed by the available options on the market. While the following models vary in fuel type, electrical load, and intended use, each one earned a spot on this lineup by excelling in real-world tests.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4.5/5; Noise 4/5; Portability 4/5; Power 5/5; Value 5/5

This Champion 7,500-watt dual-fuel generator delivers enough electricity to power up an off-grid jobsite or keep critical home circuits running through a utility outage. It includes numerous convenience features, like a push-button electric starter, covered outlets, and a fold-out transport handle. In our tests, the push-button starter worked perfectly, no matter the weather.

We appreciated its two locking 30A outlets (one 120V and one 120/240V) for powering a home, workshop, RV, or heavy-duty tools like portable welders. It also has four household-style 120V-20A outlets for lower-wattage applications. On a 7.7-gallon tank of gas, this generator supports a 50 percent load for up to 10 hours or up to 5.5 hours with a 20-pound propane cylinder. Using propane, we ran a portable table saw, miter saw, router, and air compressor for 4 hours and had just under half a tank of propane left over.

On the downside, its heavy weight made it tough to navigate across a yard and lift in and out of a truck. It also does not include an idle controller, which leads to more fuel consumption than eco-throttle-equipped generators. Still, it offered plenty of power and access points for the price.

Get the Champion 7500-watt home generator at Blain’s Farm & Fleet.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4/5; Power 4.5/5; Portability 5/5; Noise 4/5; Value 4.5/5

A no-frills dual-fuel generator that’s small enough to tuck away in the corner of a garage or in a truck bed, the Pulsar 2200-watt generator delivered an easy experience powering our test equipment. The 1.18-gallon generator can run for up to 8 hours on half load on a single tank of gas.

Setting up the generator for the first time was fairly straightforward, though filling the oil for the first time proved to be a challenge. The side panel that we needed to unscrew and pop off to access the oil fill port and dipstick was difficult to remove. We also had to lay the generator on its side to fill it with oil, which required a bit of balancing as it’s not completely flat. Balancing while pouring oil was a bit tricky, to say the least. Starting it was a piece of cake. Turning on the Pulsar requires pulling a recoil start, but the engine kicked on after just a couple of pulls—even in the cold.

We used several appliances, including a couple of heat guns and hair dryers at the same time to test the generator at different loads. As promised, the generator handled a combined 1,800 watts without much issue and shut power off when we overloaded it with 2,300 watts.

The Pulsar’s noise level seemed louder than expected, but it was still in a reasonable range for its output. It’s a great option for budget-priced backup power for light-duty power needs such as small DIY projects and powering a couple of household appliances during an outage.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 5/5; Noise 4/5; Portability 4/5; Power 4.5/5; Value 4.5/5

It’s hard to beat Westinghouse’s 4,500-watt inverter generator for power output, versatility, and portability. It’s not the highest-wattage model we tested, but it excelled at running several power tools, including grinders and saws, as well as the essentials in our travel trailer. Setting it up was a snap—all we had to do was add oil and fill the gas tank. Best of all, it’s quiet and efficient.

While this would be a great emergency generator, we also think it’s a top-notch pick for RVs, camping, and remote powering due to its quiet and efficient operation.

The iGen4500 has a 3.4-gallon gasoline tank that can run up to 18 hours. Since it’s an inverter, it only burns enough to cover the appliances or tools it’s powering. This makes it more eco-friendly than noninverter models that burn fuel at a set rate no matter how much power is being drawn. This backup generator offers a variety of power ports, including a 30A port, two 20A ports, and two USB ports. However, it weighs almost 105 pounds without fuel, so lifting it up and down can be challenging for one person.

Read our full review: Westinghouse iGen4500 Inverter Generator

Get the Westinghouse home generator at Amazon, Tractor Supply Co., or Walmart.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 5/5; Noise 4/5; Portability 4/5; Power 5/5; Value 4/5

The Honda EU2200i inverter generator offers reliability, simple operation, and low noise. Honda estimates that the EU2200i runs at 48 to 57 weighted decibels, and we found that its sound easily gets lost in the background. It comes equipped with a Honda GXR120 commercial series engine that delivers 120 volts and up to 1,800 running watts and 2,200 starting watts. A 125V-20A duplex outlet allows users to plug in two electrical cords without worrying about overloading the generator. T

his machine is compatible with the Honda My Generator mobile app for remote monitoring and is parallel-ready for use with a second Honda EU2200i (connection kit sold separately). It also features a built-in carbon monoxide sensor and safety shutoff. In two separate tests, we used the Honda generator to power a full-size refrigerator for 4 hours and then a portable table saw and miter saw for an 8-hour workday. We even added a USB hub to charge a phone and laptop.

In each test, the devices worked perfectly, the electrical current remained strong and steady, and the breaker didn’t trip. The generator’s molded plastic handle is well placed, and when combined with the machine’s proportions, the unit is fairly easy to carry, even though it weighs nearly 50 pounds. We would have appreciated a built-in USB port in addition to the wall-style outlet, though. As it was, we had to unplug a tool that draws 10A just to charge a device using less than 3A.

Still, a clean electrical current and quiet operation in a lightweight, easy-to-stow package make the Honda an excellent choice for a home backup generator.

Get the Honda home generator at Amazon or Northern Tool + Equipment.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 4.5/5; Power 5/5; Portability 4/5; Noise 4/5; Value 4.5/5

Westinghouse WGen9500DF is an all-around top pick when the power goes out. It has a 6.6-gallon gasoline tank that will keep it running for 12 hours at 25 percent load and up to 8 hours at 50 percent load. (Actual tank runtime may vary depending on gasoline quality and electrical load.) With a 20-pound propane tank, the generator will run up to 7 hours at 50 percent load.

This is a heavy model, weighing 211 pounds, but it mainly came assembled—all we had to do was attach the wheels and the handle. We tested it first with gasoline, followed by propane, and the results were excellent with both fuels. We ran several power tools simultaneously and powered an oven/range and a clothes dryer (at separate times). A nice perk was the remote fob, which allowed us to start the generator from 90 feet away.

The WGen9500DF is a good pick for those who want an emergency generator to power more than just a couple of cell phones.

Get the Westinghouse home generator at Amazon.

Our Ratings: Ease of Use 5/5; Noise 5/5; Portability 3.5/5; Power 4.5/5; Value 5/5

A distinct downside with fuel-powered generators is the inability to run them indoors due to hazardous fumes. Power stations, however, can be used indoors to power several appliances and electronic items, depending on their capacity. The Generac GB2000 is a beefy power backup that provides up to 1,600 running watts of electricity and comes with three standard outlets, four USB ports, and a 12V DC car port. Charging the GB2000 took about 3.5 hours when we plugged it directly into a standard AC outlet.

We also charged it with solar panels, but the day was slightly overcast, which kept it from charging quickly—it took about 5 hours to charge from 28 to 100 percent. A sunnier day or stronger solar panels would likely have charged it more quickly. When fully charged, we used it to run our fridge, a miter saw, lamps, a laptop, and cell phone—not all simultaneously. We made about 20 cuts with the miter saw and noticed no lag in the saw’s power. It took a couple of hours to fully charge the cell phone by placing it on the wireless charging panel at the top of the GB2000.

We left the fridge and laptop plugged in for several hours without depleting the battery—we still had 20 percent when we unplugged them. The Generac is well suited as an emergency backup for essential appliances or a remote power source for tailgating and camping, but at 43 pounds, it’s a bit heavy if you plan to carry it very far on trails.

Get the Generac home generator at The Home Depot or Walmart.

Before testing, we carefully researched more than 40 portable and standby generators and over a dozen high-capacity power stations. We looked at consumer satisfaction, ease of use, price, and versatility. We also considered brand reputation—manufacturers like Champion, Honda, Westinghouse, and Briggs & Stratton are highly respected in the generator industry.

Our goal was to pinpoint models that would suit diverse user needs. This list includes powerful, gas-fueled, and dual-fueled generators in addition to quieter inverters and even solar power stations.

Team members assembled and fueled the generators using the appropriate fuel types. Then we plugged in power tools, home appliances, and electronic devices to see if the generators would produce the advertised wattage. We used an online wattage calculator to help us estimate the correct wattage for testing. In addition, we took note of the noise the generators produced when operating (some models are quieter than others), and we took portability into account.

For each generator, we awarded points based on quality and performance. At the end of testing, we averaged the points to determine which models were best suited to specific applications and users.

Several factors can affect each type of generator’s overall functionality when the power goes out. Keep the following considerations in mind when searching for the best home generator to keep you powered up when severe weather or a natural disaster causes an outage.

Finding the best generator for your power needs involves weighing the four types available. Each type has its own set of pros and cons, and one is likely to suit your home’s specific needs better than the others.

Home standby generators, also called whole-house generators, are large, permanently installed generators that connect to a home’s electrical panel for backup power during an electrical outage. They are equipped with an automatic transfer switch (explained in later sections) that automatically switches on the generator’s power supply when it detects a loss of utility power. Home standby generators are most commonly powered by natural gas or propane, but some diesel models are available.

Home standby generators have the largest power ratings and are usually, though not always, powerful enough to satisfy the electrical demands of an entire home. They can power utilities such as water heaters and central air conditioners. The downside is that they’re the most expensive to purchase and install and require installation by certified professionals. An electrician is needed to connect it to the electrical panel, and a plumber may be required to connect the propane or natural gas line to the generator.

As the name suggests, portable generators are small and can be moved to different locations. Compared to standby options, these are cheap home generators. They can have a wide range of power output ratings. Some may only power a limited number of electrical items and appliances, while large-capacity portable generators may have a 30A outlet for connecting a transfer switch.

Many options are gas-powered generators, but some are powered by diesel or propane. Multifuel generators (dual- and tri-fuel) are powered by multiple fuel sources, including gasoline, diesel, propane, and/or natural gas. Be advised that fuel-powered generators should never be used indoors since the engine can emit a toxic level of CO, leading to sickness or death.

Inverter generators are technically a type of portable generator, but they offer a different set of pros and cons. As opposed to conventional generator engines that constantly operate at the same output rate, inverter generators adapt to power demand.

This makes them more fuel efficient, allowing them to have smaller fuel tanks and more portability than other generators. This efficiency reduces their CO emissions. It also makes them quiet generators, usually only producing around a 60-decibel noise level—the same volume level as a typical conversation.

All of these advantages make them a popular choice among campers and RVers who value silent operation and portability. The disadvantages? Inverter generators are typically more expensive than regular portable generators and generally produce less power.

Portable power stations are essentially battery banks that store electrical power. Instead of using gasoline, diesel, propane, or natural gas, power stations are charged in a standard electrical outlet or via solar panels. Solar-powered power stations are often called solar generators and offer a practically unlimited and renewable power source—just as long as the sun is shining.

These power stations are usually designed to be compact and lightweight for incredible portability. They are also virtually silent when in operation. In addition, they can be safely used indoors since they don’t produce any harmful emissions.

The primary disadvantages are that they’re usually more expensive than conventional portable generators and have significantly lower power output. That being said, they can be used to power a handful of essential appliances during an outage.

A generator’s size equates to the amount of power in watts it can generate, ranging from 800 to greater than 50,000-watt power capacity. Generators are sized in both “peak watts” (also called “starting watts”) and “running watts,” but the running watts rating reveals how many continuous watts the generator can supply to keep the electricity running to the connected items. Peak watts are the number of watts the generator can produce for a few seconds to satisfy the extra demand from starting motor-driven items like power tools.

The running watts rating dictates the number of electrical appliances you can run simultaneously and for how long. Because a generator’s starting watts can be 1,000 to 2,000 watts greater than its running watts, it’s important to understand the difference when determining your home’s power requirements.

To determine the generator size you need, calculate the total number of watts your most essential electrical items consume. All electrical appliances are required to have a label that displays their wattage rating; you simply need to add together the wattage figure listed on each item you want to power during an electrical outage.

Generally speaking, a refrigerator consumes between 700 and 1,200 watts; a window air conditioner consumes around 1,000 watts; a portable space heater consumes about 1,500 watts; and lights can be anywhere between 60 and 600 watts. Most households typically require around 5,000 watts to power the most essential components.

A transfer switch connects a generator to a home’s main electrical panel and allows you to switch, or “transfer,” between grid power and generator power. There are both manual and automatic transfer switches. Manual transfer switches require you to physically switch over the power at your electrical panel when the power goes out, while an automatic transfer switch automatically switches over power from the grid to generator power when it detects a loss of power.

Generators can be equipped with various features that make their operation as convenient as possible. These include:

Along with the various features that contribute to convenience, there are also a few important safety features that many generators incorporate into their design, particularly with regard to CO detection. These include:

Operating a generator isn’t complicated, but there are a few tips you’ll want to keep in mind to ensure effective and safe operation and avoid any harm to yourself or your generator when the power goes out.

First of all, it’s important to use extension cords that are sufficiently powerful. As a general rule, use at least 12-gauge outdoor-rated electrical cords for larger appliances (like refrigerators) and at least 14-gauge cords for everything else. Cord length is also a concern since the voltage drop from cords that are too long can damage or destroy the appliances they’re supplying. Although you’ll want to keep a portable generator outside and away from your home, remember not to exceed a 100-foot extension cord length.

When running a home generator, it’s best to keep it on a level surface. If a generator is placed on a slope or hill, the internal lubrication system could malfunction and lead to premature engine wear and damage. Finally, ensure the generator has cooled down before you refill it since spilling gasoline on a hot engine can cause a fire.

Since generators can be both lifesaving and expensive, it’s natural to have some questions before you’re confident in your decision. To this end, consider the answers to these frequently asked questions. Together with the above shopping considerations, these answers provide you with just about everything you need to know about emergency power generators.

Most municipalities and towns require that you have a permit before a standby generator is installed. However, not all locations have this requirement. Check with your local zoning authority to find out if a permit is required in your community.

To determine the necessary power rating for your generator, add up the wattage of all the appliances you want to run during an electrical outage. As a general rule, a generator should have at least a 5,000-watt capacity to power a home’s essential appliances. But, your home could require substantially more power than that.

Yes, fuel-powered home generators require oil to operate properly without wearing out the moving parts.

Most manufacturers recommend that the whole-house generator be installed at least 5 feet from doors, windows, vents, and any flammable material. There may also be local regulations about the placement of a whole-house generator, so make sure to check local guidelines to ensure that your whole-house generator meets local building codes and installation guidelines.

No. A generator should not be plugged into a house outlet because it is both dangerous and illegal in many locations. This is called backfeeding and it can severely damage the home’s electrical system or even cause a fire. Additionally, any technicians who are working on electrical lines on the same power grid are at increased risk of electrocution.

You can connect a generator to individual appliances using a series of extension cords, or if you want direct power for entire circuits in the home, have a standby generator professionally installed.

On average, it will take an experienced technician about 1 to 2 full days to complete the installation of your whole-house generator. It could take 1 to 2 weeks to prep for the installation, such as pouring a concrete pad and running the wires connecting the generator to the electrical panel.

The exact lifespan depends on the specific product, but in general, the overall lifespan of a whole-house generator is about 1,500 to 3,000 hours.

Every SpongeBob SquarePants Christmas & Winter Episode

📅 Dec 04, 2020  |  Category: Science  |  Read Source →

SpongeBob SquarePants' Christmas episodes show that living in tropical waters isn't a barrier to winter adventures, and the best SpongeBob Christmas episodes also rank among the show's funniest so far. Nickelodeon has consistently delivered some of the best animated TV shows of all time for decades, and, by far, its most popular and successful cartoon is SpongeBob SquarePants. Thanks to its unique characters, setting, fun situations, and different types of comedy, SpongeBob SquarePants has maintained its popularity and relevance for over two decades.

Since 1999, SpongeBob SquarePants has taken viewers to the bottom of the ocean, to the town of Bikini Bottom, to meet the title character and his friends. Throughout 17 seasons, SpongeBob has gotten involved in all types of fun and absurd situations, but living under the sea doesn’t mean that SpongeBob and company don’t get to celebrate the same holidays as those living on land. SpongeBob SquarePants has had various holiday-themed episodes, from Halloween to Christmas and more, but the episodes set in the latter holiday have been the most popular.

While there aren’t many SpongeBob SquarePants episodes dedicated specifically to the spirit of Christmas, it does have a few winter-themed stories that fit perfectly with the holiday. Leaving aside the logic and physics of snow underwater, SpongeBob, Patrick, and even Squidward often find their unique brand of festive magic, while sticking to the absurdist humor the show is known for.

"Christmas Who?" is the first SpongeBob SquarePants Christmas special. In it, Sandy is decorating her tree and gearing up for Christmas. SpongeBob and Patrick have never heard of Christmas and learn everything about it from Sandy. SpongeBob does what he does best and gets overexcited and spreads the word around Bikini Bottom.

The city becomes a Christmas wonderland, but when Santa fails to appear on Christmas morning, Squidward takes it upon himself to become Santa and give cheer to Bikini Bottom. This really shares the meaning of Christmas, with the nice twist that it's Squidward, of all the characters, who gets most into the holiday.

SpongeBob and Patrick go to Sandy's house to play, but find her entire dome covered in a shield with a sign that says "Keep out." The two sneak in to find Sandy asleep, but she doesn't look like herself — she is much larger and fuzzier. Sandy is hibernating for the winter, but SpongeBob and Patrick play in her snow-filled tree dome anyway.

While playing in the snow seems enjoyable and gives this episode a Christmas spirit, the noise wakes Sandy up, and she’s not pleased. SpongeBob and Patrick end up paying the price for being too loud, and they also begin to struggle with the cold.

"Snowball Effect" shows Bikini Bottom under a blanket of snow after an iceberg crashes above. SpongeBob and Patrick spend the episode having a snowball fight, but things take a turn when Squidward gets involved. The three build forts to protect themselves from the snowballs, but Squidward goes too far and realizes he is having more fun than he thought he would.

The snow makes this a Christmas-themed episode, even though it is from an iceberg, but the sight of Squidward playing with SpongeBob and Patrick makes this one of the most enjoyable winter/holiday episodes in the show's history.

"Krabs Á La Mode" shows Mr. Krabs turning the Krusty Krab into an unintentional ice rink for winter. For Squidward, turning down the heat during the frigid temperatures in Bikini Bottom is one of the worst things Mr. Krabs could have done. Due to expenses, Mr. Krabs sets an alarm at the Krusty Krab so no one can turn up the heat and turns the Krusty Krab into an ice rink, complete with Krusty Krab-themed skates and activities.

"Frozen Face-Off" celebrates winter with a sled competition, and everyone in Bikini Bottom enters because the prize is $1 million. SpongeBob has Gary leading his sled, Patrick chooses gummy worms to take him to victory, and Squidward has a worm who chooses to sip tea instead of winning the race.

While everyone tries to win the prize, Plankton is busy trying to steal the Krabby Patty secret recipe. When the gang tries to stop him, they're frozen in ice, but the ever-resourceful Sandy makes a fire to warm everyone up, ending the episode on a festively-themed note of the value of friendship and cooperation.

"It's a SpongeBob Christmas" is, arguably, the best SpongeBob Christmas episode. To celebrate the holidays, this episode shows Bikini Bottom excited about Santa's arrival. As always, Plankton has an evil plan up his sleeve to ruin Christmas cheer, so he puts all of Bikini Bottom on Santa's naughty list.

What makes this episode stand out is the fact that it’s not traditionally animated like other SpongeBob SquarePants Christmas episodes. Instead, the episode is animated in claymation and pays homage to Christmas claymation classics.

In this episode, Squidward tries to sell his art at the Krusty Krab, but when Mr. Krabs shuts it down, he sends Squidward to Antarctica to bring his art to a big-time buyer. Once there, Squidward comes across a snow sponge that looks a lot like a snowman.

As it turns out, SpongeBob is (somehow) already in Antarctica eating ice by the time Squidward gets there. This episode brings Squidward and SpongeBob to a snowy terrain that is unlike what they normally experience in Bikini Bottom, with the change of locale being a clever way to tap into the Christmas themes.

"Shopping List" doesn't include Santa or snowball fights, but it does include a cold-living yeti. In the episode, Mr. Krabs gives SpongeBob an intense shopping list with odd things that aren't easily found. Sandy decides to help SpongeBob since she is braver than him, especially when it comes to getting the hair of a Yeti.

Sandy and SpongeBob get the job done, but the Yeti eventually tracks them down to get its hair back. It offers a small touch of winter magic that enters Bikini Bottom, along with one of winter's most mythical creatures.

“Goons on the Moon” sees Sandy taking her Science Scout troop on a trip to the moon. While they all go their separate ways to explore, Santa Claus is revealed to be sleeping on the dark side of the moon. When Santa realizes how far Earth is from the moon, they all try to get it closer but end up destroying the moon.

Santa then pumps SpongeBob full of water, turning him into a replacement moon, with Santa promising to deliver a new one in December. SpongeBob SquarePants has become weirder and weirder with every season, but at least this episode acknowledges SpongeBob’s past encounters with Santa Claus.

"Plankton's Old Chum" shows Plankton creating a fake holiday. Plankton realizes he has nowhere to dump his old chum, so when he runs into SpongeBob, he convinces him that it's Chum Day, a holiday similar to Christmas. On this day, a person hands out chum as a gift, and SpongeBob falls for it, taking Plankton's old chum and spreading it around Bikini Bottom. When the city becomes angry over the filth, SpongeBob convinces them it's a holiday, showing he can turn anything into a festive celebration.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Best Gifts and Car Accessories for Auto-Obsessed Teens and New Drivers

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

Shopping for a teenage auto enthusiast or a just-licensed driver? Here are some fantastic gift ideas they'll love.

This article was updated in November 2026 with new products and information.

Finding the perfect gift can be quite the challenge, especially when the person you're shopping for is a teenager whose interests seem to change with the weather. But fear not! Whether your teen is a fledging automotive enthusiast, a tech wizard, or has just acquired their license, we've assembled a handful of great gift ideas they're bound to love.

From new-age electronics to awesome interior upgrades, these finds are sure to impress. We’ve also included a handful of items focused on protection and preparedness—because keeping them safe while on the road is just as important as fueling their passions.

7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

📅 Jul 20, 2020  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room will steal the show in any home tour. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Between them, they cover most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multi-toned colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant’s inspiration, Noguchi’s Akaris, hang in many an AD home tour, including over the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant light can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whateveryou’re cheffing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional spaces in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26" and 32" sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this hand-woven rattan pendant over a kitchen island stands to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; This single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: he wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay pick uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

The Plant Doctor Is In: How to Save a Dying Houseplant

📅 Apr 17, 2020  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

You saw a plant you loved in a shop or nursery. You brought it home, and for months it warmed your heart. But then it started to decline. What are you doing wrong? Is it dying? Can you save it?

For answers, we talked with the plant doctor: Christopher Satch, who has a master’s degree in botany from Rutgers University, teaches plant science at the New York Botanic Garden, and is involved with the Manhattan Orchid Society and the American Orchid Society. Oh, and he’s also the plant scientist for The Sill, which has two shops in Manhattan and ships houseplants nationwide.

As the plant doctor, Chris fields calls and email from people seeking advice about ailing houseplants; he also dispenses knowledge during weekly drop-in clinics at The Sill’s Upper West Side shop. The best way to keep a plant happy, he says, is to learn what it wants before you buy it—and only buy plants that suit the conditions you can offer. If you live in a ground-floor apartment with little light, say, don’t get a plant that will only thrive with tons of direct sunshine.

How can you diagnose a dying plant’s problem in time to cure it? “A lot of symptoms overlap with many causes,” says Chris. “You have to piece together the puzzle to diagnose the problem.” Here’s how to cure your plant’s ailment.

Symptoms: “If the leaves are turning yellow, the soil is moist, and you can see fungus growing at the base, you’re overwatering,” says Chris. Customers are sternly warned about how often their new plants will need water.

Solution: “Keep cacti and succulents in the sun, which cooks them dry very fast—that’s what they like,” Chris says. “Give these plants a dry rest, maybe a week, and then water them. Shade plants like ferns want their soil to be moist for a little while. Let them approach dryness—but then hit them with water right away.”

Prevention: No plant likes its roots sitting in water, which is why the pot needs good drainage—either a hole in the bottom or a layer of lava rocks or recycled terracotta shards (they’re porous, so they absorb excess water and slowly release it).

Symptoms: If leaves look droopy and are falling off, it’s a good sign that the plant’s not getting enough water.

Solution: “The goal is to saturate the soil so it’s evenly moist, and then let it dry out before watering again,” says Chris. “Most indoor plants are tropical, and they like warm water, not hot or cold.”

Chris outlines two basic watering techniques. The first: “Pour a little water into the center of the pot, let it sink in, pour a little more, and keep doing that until the soil is saturated.” How much water in all? A good rule of thumb is about a quarter to a third of the pot’s volume.

The second technique: soaking. “Put the potted plant in a sink or bowl and pour water slowly onto the top of the soil. Keep going until about a half inch of water has gone through the pot and collected in the sink or bowl. Then let the plant soak—maybe even a full day. Take it out and let it drip dry before putting it back in its planter or plate.” (This method only works when the pot has a drainage hole in the bottom—it can even be plastic.)

Prevention: “Sometimes people just dump water on the plant and it immediately flows through and comes out the bottom,” says Chris. “Your plant has not been watered—if you stick your finger into the soil you’ll see that the center of the root ball is still bone dry. That’s why soaking is important.”

Tip: That trick about putting ice cubes in the pot so the water is released slowly? Forget it.

Symptoms: You can blast most indoor plants with light and they’ll be fine, because the sun is much weaker when it comes through a window. But plants that prefer low light, like ferns and calathea, will be burned by too much sun—blanched leaves are a sign. On the other hand, plants that are not getting enough light may get spindly and stretched out or start dropping leaves, says Chris.

Solution: Most houseplants prefer medium to bright light. “Think of the fiddle-leaf fig, which people plant as outdoor hedges in Florida. They love the sun, and they’ll drop leaves in response to lower light.”

Prevention: Artificial light may help. If the natural light in your home is really low, artificial light can make plants happier. No bulb offers the full spectrum of light that plants need—not even the ones designed as grow lights—but using a more intense bulb will increase the effect. Chris recommends an LED or CFL (compact fluorescent) bulb (screw-in, not tubes), as long as it’s 1,000 lumens or more; either warm or cool is fine.

Symptoms: Too much fertilizer and “your plant will get crispy edges, called salt burn, and the leaves may turn brown or black,” Chris says. “Basically, the plant will start falling apart very fast.” But too little fertilizer may cause a plant to stop thriving;  fertilizer is important for longevity. “When a plant grows in nature, the soil is infinite; the roots can keep stretching out to find new nutrients,” Chris says. “But in a pot, that’s their universe and you’re essentially their god. Once the nutrients have been exhausted, the plant says ‘Uh-oh, what am I going to do?’

Solution: “You have to be their savior, and add fertilizer to provide nutrients—it’s literally a multivitamin for the plant,” says Chris. Follow the instructions on the label.

Prevention: Use crystals, liquid, or slow-release pellets as recommended.

Symptoms:  Chris says customers email him asking, Why isn’t my plant growing? “I look at the photo and write back, ‘Do you not notice how big the plant is in relation to the pot?’ It’s like trying to jam your foot into a shoe that’s two sizes too small!”

Solution: A rule of thumb is that the volume of the plant should be two-thirds above ground and one-third below ground. The soil level should be within an inch or two of the rim, depending on the pot size, so when you pour in water it can pool before seeping in.

Prevention: When you buy a plant that comes in a plastic pot, repot it right away. “Plants are sold overgrown; they’re not meant to live in those pots. They need room to grow,” says Chris.

Tip: Don’t let your soil get too old. “Potting mix does decay and get mucky over time,” says Chris. Even if you’re fertilizing regularly, swap out some of the soil every year or two. It’s also a good opportunity to see if your plant needs a larger pot.

Symptoms: Dry air is very bad, causing plants to crisp their leaves, says Chris.

Solution:  Keep plants well away from vents, heaters, radiators, and air conditioners. To combat low humidity, he offers three pieces of advice: “Humidifier, humidifier, humidifier.” Get the largest one you can—it’s great for human health as well. If you only have a dinky one, put it right next to the plants.

Prevention: Misting can help if it’s done regularly, but as soon as the mist dries, the effect is gone. You can also group plants with similar needs to create a microclimate—but note that if you get pests, every plant will be infested.

Symptoms: Mealy bugs look like tiny white cotton balls. Other bugs look like, well, bugs.

Solution: “Using an insecticide like Raid is like using an atom bomb to knock down a house,” says Chris. “And those chemicals can hurt the plant.” He recommends washing the plant with warm water, then wiping it down to remove as many bugs as possible. Follow up with a spray of horticultural oil or insecticidal soap, being careful to reach the crevices and the undersides of the leaves.

Prevention: Keep your plant clean (dust or wipe its leaves gently) and check it regularly for bugs.

“Most of the time those little plastic tags you get with a plant are useless,” says Chris. Ask the person you buy it from; a good nursery should know what they’re selling. Otherwise you can always contact Chris at The Sill, search online, or send your question (with a photo) to your local botanic garden.

“If it’s brown or looks dead, it probably is dead,” says Chris. But some woody plants, like the rubber tree and the fiddle-leaf fig, can recover even if they’ve died back to the stalk. “Blast it with light, give it a little fertilizer, water it when it’s dry, and it’ll say ‘Life is worth living! Let’s regrow!’ You could have the same luck with plants like pothos and monstera, as long as there’s a little vine or stem left.”

On the other hand, if a plant’s too far gone—and definitely not beautifying your home—it can be wise to cut your losses and start again with something fresh.

“Most indoor plants will do absolutely fine on a steady diet of dappled sun and water when they’re dry,” Chris says reassuringly. “Cacti and succulents need more bright light and dry soil; ferns need wetter conditions. And then there’s Sansevieria, which deserves all the respect—whether it’s under artificial light or blasted with direct sun, it’ll just keep growing.”

For more houseplant growing tips, see our curated guide to Houseplants 101: A Field Guide to Planting, Care & Design, including Prayer Plants, Fiddle-Leaf Fig Trees, and Monstera:
