Each fit brings its own flavor to the table, so whether you’re going for a sleek, body-conscious look or maximum comfort on a lazy day, there’s a T-shirt out there that’s up your alley. Here, we break down the most common cuts to consider.

This tee is tailored to follow your body’s lines without feeling snug. Go for this if you want your tee to show off your hard-earned gains.

Classic fit tees offer a balanced cut that isn’t too tight or too loose. They drape nicely over any frame, delivering a no-fuss, everyday look that looks great under a blazer, bomber jacket, or on its own on casual Fridays. It’s a reliable go-to when you want to keep your fit simple yet sharp.

When comfort is your mantra, a relaxed fit is where it’s at. These tees give you plenty of room to breathe and move.

Built for the guy with a muscular build, athletic fit tees have broader shoulders and a narrower waist. They’re engineered to give your jacked upper body the space it needs without turning you into a walking grocery bag.

100% cotton tees are often the gold standard for everyday tees, but cotton often doesn’t perform the best, which is why tech-blended fabrics can give useful features like moisture-wicking, four-way stretch, and breathability. Hence, if you're an active guy, or maybe you sweat a lot naturally, or are looking for tees for going out, ditch the full cotton and opt for blended fabrics. Below is an overview of the kinds of materials to choose from.

The classic choice. Think: breathable, soft, and natural cotton. Whether it's combed, ring-spun, or Pima, pure cotton tees just get better with each wash.

All the comfort of regular cotton, but grown without harsh chemicals. It's eco-friendly and feels extra gentle on your skin.

When cotton teams up with polyester or rayon, you get a tee that's more durable, resists shrinkage, and sometimes even wrinkles less. It's all about that low-maintenance vibe.

These are designed for action. Performance blends often feature polyester or moisture-wicking fibers to keep you cool and dry. Whether you're hitting the gym or commuting, these tees move with you and manage sweat like a champ.

Different styles of tees work best for different occasions. For instance, a V-neck T-shirt works best as an undershirt and can make your neck look longer. A crewneck is your everyday kind of tee that’s great for layering or on its own. Garment-dyed tees look lived-in for a vintage style. And if you want to look dressy in T-shirts, ditch the plain-looking ones that look like undershirts and go for a pocketed tee instead. Better yet, opt for graphic tees to really make a style statement that is true to you.

To find the best t-shirts for men, our style team led by Style and Reviews Director, Christian Gollayan has personally tried on over 60 different options since 2020 and tested each of them for at least two weeks by wearing them in all sorts of situations. We vetted our tested options by making notes of their style, type, comfort, fit, price point, durability, versatility, and performance such as stretch, moisture-wicking, and breathability.

We examined whether they fit well and true to size, whether their fabric could withstand hours of movement in different temperatures, how the collar stood up after multiple wears, and whether they could be worn for more than one occasion. We also tested these T-shirts on different body types, from slim to athletic to buff, to ensure the most unbiased results.

Specifically, we're looking for T-shirt styles that are not trendy or trend-chasing (so no funky graphics, baggy fits, or holes here); instead, our picks for the best t-shirts for men all fit great even on buff guys, provide a ton of value at reasonable price points, and come in styles that will look great years from now. The most popular and reputable T-shirt brands for men that consistently deliver high-quality tees often happen to be the best clothing brands for men. For instance, Calvin Klein, J.Crew, and Sunspel often churn out classic tees in premium materials suitable for both casual and smart-casual occasions. Buck Mason and Billy Reid are known for making casual pocketed tees with a lived-in vibe and garment-dyed fabric.

Athleisure brands like Vuori, Lululemon, and Rhone make t-shirts that are versatile for all kinds of physical activities. Trendy brands like CDLP, Everlane, and Richer Poorer are what you should go to for relaxed-fit, oversized tees. For undershirts or tees for homebodies, Tommy John, Jockey, Calvin Klein, and Hanes have you covered.

Our all-time favorite, tried-and-tested t-shirt brand, however, is Mack Weldon, which makes tees that are soft, versatile, long-lasting, well-fitted, and can do no wrong.

That depends on the occasion and personal style. For a casual look, you can't go wrong with a classic crew neck T-shirt (especially those that have gone through the wash to look more vintage and lived-in). V-necks add a touch of flair and work great as undershirts. Graphic tees let you showcase your personality, while T-shirts with a pocket let you look a bit more sophisticated. For athletic activities, moisture-wicking and performance t-shirts are a no-brainer when you go to the gym or hike.

Providing the right care to your tees will help keep them looking sharp and feeling comfy for eons. First up, read the damn label! Make sure you follow the washing instructions. Also, be sure to separate colors when you wash T-shirts (i.e. darks with darks and lights with lights).

When you wash, make sure the water is cold because the temperature will help preserve colors and prevent shrinkage. Turning T-shirts inside out can also help protect the outer fabric from friction and fading. Use a gentle cycle and mild detergent to help maintain the shape and fabric, too.

Additionally, skip the dryer and air-dry if possible because high heat can cause shrinkage (use low-heat settings if you must air-dry). We also recommend folding instead of hanging T-shirts because the latter will stretch out the neck of the tees and cause them to lose their shape.

These are two indicators of whether your T-shirt can or should be tucked in or left untucked. With a curved hem, then the tee is best left untucked; it'll probably look better that way. A straight hem can do both, depending on the length. That is to say, a longer length is thus best left tucked in, unless you've got the height or the style magic to offset any disproportion.

Don’t mistake them for only for “beginners.” Trickle-down design means these cheap trainers are more capable than ever for tough workouts.

Editor’s Note, November 19, 2026: Black Friday sales are in full swing. Several of our already low-priced trainers are discounted for as high as 30% off, including our Best Overall, the New Balance 680v9.

“All you need is a pair of shoes to start running” isn’t exactly truthful. If you hate your toenails and heels, by all means, wear Converse high-tops. A good pair of running shoes will spare your digits and the skin on your feet—but that doesn’t mean you have to drop cash on expensive trainers.

Cheap running shoes, models less than $130, are a respectable choice for beginners and vets wanting to save. From plush to moderately cushioned, we’ve listed affordable running shoes on this list that’ll help you save up for more run gear, like a fancy GPS watch or that next destination race.

To find the best shoes under $130, we performed comprehensive research on top-rated trainers by well-known brands, and called in shoes for our shoe program testers and staff to evaluate. These shoes were vetted by scrutinizing fit, ride, and comfort. To give you both options, we included our premium recommendation for each shoe from our Best Running Shoes buying guide, just in case you want to spend some extra cash.

🏃 For even more choices, head to The Best Running Shoes of 2026

We also spoke with Footwear Buyer Jon Notary, who works at the local running store Aardvark Sports Shop in Bethlehem, Pennsylvania. Notary provides insight on what you’re compromising on when buying cheap shoes, and gives advice on what to look for when you’re out shopping for your next pair.

Buying an affordable trainer doesn’t necessarily mean subpar features. Brands will sometimes incorporate “old” technology into these shoes, like midsole foams, to mitigate a high price. But you are sacrificing some premium specs, like plush cushioning and more durable materials.

Below is a comparison chart of our Best Overall budget trainer, the New Balance Fresh Foam 680v9, versus the brand’s premium daily trainer, the Fresh Foam X 880v15. The latter has the next generation of Fresh Foam, Fresh Foam X, and more cushioning. You save almost half the money with the 680v9, but you get what you paid for with the 880v15.

The 680v9 is still a reliable shoe for everyday runs and light workouts. If you’re an efficient runner (you don’t wear down your shoes’ soles faster than the average runner), you may even get more mileage out of the 680v9s. It comes down to what you’re willing to give up and what you’re willing to spend.

All the trainers on this list are Best Value material, but the 680v9 deserves the title for its consistency with each update. Though not as plush or responsive as, say, the more expensive 1080 or 880, the 680 still encapsulates New Balance’s soft-cushioned feel, absorbing shock and promoting comfort on every run.

Its Fresh Foam midsole provides a supportive, plush, lightweight ride, making the 680v9 an excellent choice as an everyday trainer that can handle base-building mileage. A breathable, cozy upper makes this a comfy running shoe and great for casual wear, as well.

Despite her distaste for the Launch 12’s tightness, she praised the shoe’s lightweight and responsiveness. Tysiac found the shoe’s simplicity refreshing in our max-cushioned era of trainers.

One knock is the shoe is quite heavy, and it runs a little small and narrow, so consider sizing up and trying on the wide version.

Shop Men’s at amazon.com | Shop Women’s at amazon.com

According to Notary, the two main sacrifices you make when buying cheaper running shoes are comfort and longevity. That doesn’t mean you’re stuck with choosing uncomfortable shoes when you’re searching for cheaper pairs. It just means you have to do the extra, er, footwork, when considering more affordable shoes.

Notary notes the materials are not as premium or as durable. “That being said, the industry does benefit from what I call the technology trickle-down; many of the materials on lower priced models were once used on a ‘fancier’ one.” For example, the Brooks Launch 12 has DNA Flash v2 foam which was used in its Hyperion Elite super shoes.

“A lower cost shoe will use older foams and fabrics, possibly less outsole, or no outsole, and less involved construction techniques. All of those realities typically lead to less overall comfort than what else is currently available,” says Notary. A cheaper shoe will be comfortable, it just won’t have the same max plush and support of a top-shelf trainer.

Notary says comfort is king. “If it feels heavy, stiff, and firm in the store, don’t expect that to change. Conversely, if it feels hyper-light, super flexible, and marshmallowy soft, there is a good chance it won’t be as durable.”

Perhaps you’re leaning toward one end of the spectrum of cushioning or somewhere in the middle. The best way to find if a cheap shoe—or any running shoe—is right for you is to try it on first and go for a test run in the store. Some shoe stores allow you to run on the sidewalk or on a treadmill to see if the shoe’s a good fit.

Cheap running shoes have a shorter shelf life. “Because the materials are less premium they typically break down faster even when they’re not being worn,” says Notary. “This isn’t always true and is somewhat of a generalization, but higher grade materials generally offer more durability. If we’re considering mileage, a lower-cost shoe typically yields fewer miles.”

Notary adds, however, that some of the most expensive running shoes on the market right now, i.e., super shoes or shoes with a carbon plate, also have a shorter mileage life, which is another thing to put in perspective.

We took pains not to include discounted models on this list but shouldn’t be overlooked when buying running shoes. In some cases, the differences between last season and the current season are unnoticeable.

Beware of dupes on sites like Amazon. A usually expensive model slapped with a too-good-to-be-true price tag is probably just that. The model in question likely isn’t the shoe promised, is poorly made, and could cause injury due to poor quality materials. Check the seller to see it’s from the actual brand (and make sure it’s not someone posing as a well-known brand).

For models by lesser known brands, Notary gives us some pointers when choosing running shoes. “Things to look for are a rubber outsole and a removable footbed/liner,” he says. “These are two quick check aspects of a viable running shoe. Also, look for fewer sewn overlays on the upper, more will only cause problems with flexibility and add excessive weight to the shoe.”

Will cheap running shoes make me run slower?

In general, a cheaper shoe will not have as much pop or energy return as a more expensive one which in theory should make it slower. Lower cost shoes will likely lack plating or supercritical foams found in today’s ‘fastest’ shoes. However, a lot depends on how comfortably the shoe moves with you. Some of the ‘fastest’ shoes on the market today don’t agree with my body’s mechanics, and I find them slower than more conventional shoes in my rotation.

Are cheap running shoes a good choice for new runners?

In my world, any shoe that gets someone running is a good choice. That said, a well-fitting, comfortable technical running shoe will have a much better chance of keeping a new runner in the sport.

It’s a shame that with so much great and relatively affordable product available, many people start the sport in the wrong shoe and quit, never knowing a different reality exists for them. Unfortunately the ‘running shoe’ category is a bit of a catch-all, and there are plenty of shoes categorized as ‘running’ that have no place being labeled as such.

How old can a past shoe model be for it to be ok to run in?

Unworn shoes will definitely lose some cushioning after about two to five years due to oxidization, which hardens the midsole foam. Glue also weakens over time, increasing the risk of delamination in shoes that have been sitting around a while, primarily between the upper and the midsole and the midsole and the outsole. They can be used, sure, but they won’t have the typical 300 to 500 mile range of usability nor the comfort of a ‘fresher’ pair.

Amanda Furrer, Editor, Running Reviews, studied journalism at NYU and writing at Emerson College. She has reviewed gear and covered other topics in the running space for almost 10 years. Since 2013, she has consecutively run the Boston Marathon. She also has a master’s degree in gastronomy from Boston University and was formerly a professional baker for two years before hanging up her apron.

Dry-Brined Turkey With Tangy Honey Glaze

📅 Oct 16, 2018  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

This dry-brine turkey recipe produces a Thanksgiving centerpiece that’s full of flavor and juicy as heck. The brine is just kosher salt and brown sugar, applied at least 12 hours and up to 2 days ahead. The salt helps the meat retain moisture, while an uncovered rest in the fridge dries the skin for better browning—no bucket required.

Roasting the bird on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet (instead of a high-sided roasting pan) lets hot air circulate around it, encouraging browning all over. We’ve also done away with the turkey baster in favor of a buttery honey-vinegar glaze you’ll brush over the bird while it cooks. Fragrant with rosemary, garlic, and orange zest, with Worcestershire sauce for savory depth, the repeated brushing builds an even, glossy lacquer.

Let the cooked turkey rest uncovered for 30 minutes to 1 hour before carving. Resist the urge to tent it with foil, which traps steam and softens that hard-earned crispy skin. Use the downtime to put the finishing touches on your Thanksgiving side dishes.

What kind of turkey should I buy for dry-brining?

Choose an unseasoned turkey. Kosher, self-basting, and enhanced turkeys have already been salted, so adding this dry brine can make them overly salty. Check the label for an added salt solution before buying. A frozen turkey is fine; thaw it in the refrigerator for at least 24 hours before starting the brine.

No. In this recipe, the brine stays on the bird; Step 2 calls for rinsing the baking sheet and rack to prevent the drippings from becoming too salty, not the turkey. Follow the measurement for your brand of kosher salt, and don’t feel compelled to use every last grain of the mixture—the recipe intentionally makes extra.

Yes, but taste them first: The dry brine and glaze make these drippings especially flavorful, and they may be quite salty. Skim off the excess fat to make your roux, then add the juices a little at a time, followed by low-sodium chicken broth (or homemade unseasoned turkey stock).

Our Make-Ahead Gravy doesn’t need drippings, but you can stir some in as it reheats. Taste before adding salt.

Can I use a larger or spatchcocked turkey?

Yes, but not with the same roasting schedule. This dry brine recipe is written for a whole 12–14-lb. bird; a larger turkey or one that’s been flattened will cook differently. For a spatchcocked bird, follow our Spatchcock Turkey With Anise and Orange rather than transferring this recipe’s roasting and glazing times.

See why each step works in our guide to cooking Thanksgiving turkey →

4 hours 45 minutes, plus at least 12 hours brining

Place ½ cup Diamond Crystal or ¼ cup plus 1½ tsp. Morton kosher salt and 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar in a small bowl and work together with your fingers until incorporated. Place one 12–14-lb. turkey, neck reserved for gravy, giblets discarded, on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet and pat dry with paper towels. (If you don’t have this setup, place your turkey on a V-shape rack set inside a large roasting pan.) Sprinkle salt mixture all over outside of the turkey as well as inside the cavity of the turkey, patting to adhere and nudging some into every crevice. You won’t need all of the dry brine, but it’s good to have extra since some of it will end up on the baking sheet as you season the turkey. Chill bird, uncovered, at least 12 hours and up to 2 days.

Remove turkey from wire rack and rinse baking sheet and rack if needed (turkey will most likely release some liquid onto pan). Line baking sheet with 3 layers of foil and set rack back inside. Place turkey, breast side up, on rack and tuck wings underneath. Let turkey sit at room temperature 2–3 hours.

Place an oven rack in middle of oven; preheat oven to 450°. Using your fingers, loosen turkey skin on breast. Work 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, under skin, spreading evenly over both breasts. Smear outside of turkey with another 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature.

Tie legs together with kitchen twine and pour 1 cup water into baking sheet.

Roast turkey, rotating pan halfway through, until skin is mostly golden brown all over, about 30 minutes.

Meanwhile, cook ¼ cup sherry vinegar or red wine vinegar, 2 Tbsp. honey, 4 tsp. Worcestershire sauce, 3 sprigs rosemary, 3 garlic cloves, crushed, two 2x1" strips orange zest, and remaining 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, in a small saucepan over medium heat until bubbling and slightly thickened, about 5 minutes. Reduce heat to lowest setting and keep glaze warm.

Reduce oven temperature to 300° and continue to roast turkey, brushing with glaze every 30 minutes and adding more water by ½-cupfuls as needed to maintain some liquid in baking sheet, until an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of breast near the neck registers 155° (don’t worry; the temperature will continue to climb while the bird rests), 65–85 minutes longer. Skin should be deep golden brown, shiny, and crisp. Transfer turkey to a cutting board and let rest at least 30 minutes and up to 1 hour before carving.

Editor’s note: The dry-brine turkey recipe was first printed in our November 2018 issue as ‘Dry-Rubbed Roast Turkey.’ Head this way for more of our best Thanksgiving recipes →

The 7 Best Exercise Bikes, According to Fitness Editors and Certified Trainers

📅 Sep 10, 2018  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Skip the studio hassles and get your cardio in at home.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

IN A WORLD where home workout trends change with the seasons, the exercise bike continues to stand the test of time. Its appeal lies in its ability to increase cardiovascular health through a low-impact approach, with limitless ability to take it up a notch. With a range of indoor exercise bike styles available, from air bikes to spin bikes and even recumbent bikes, it can be difficult to choose the right style for your fitness goals and preferences.

Recent innovations in "smart" bikes have expanded the indoor bike market even further, offering a wider selection than ever before. So we leveraged the extensive collective experience of MH fitness editors, anchored by months of hands-on testing from certified trainers, to distinguish which factors set the top options apart from the rest.

Each of the bikes in this guide can help users burn calories and improve overall fitness, but certain styles diverge in fundamental ways. Spin bikes, similar to what you might find at a SoulCycle class, engage your core and legs, while air bikes require you to use your upper body as well. Air bike enthusiasts appreciate their effectiveness in high-intensity interval training and circuit-style workouts due to their ability to require quick bursts of intense effort. Meanwhile, spin bikes cater to a broader range of intensities, longer cycling sessions, and supplementing outdoor cycling routines.

To help you select the ideal exercise bike for you and your home gym, we've curated a list of the top exercise bikes we've personally tested, fitting all budget levels. From the ultra-high-end Peloton Bike+ to the most budget-friendly options available on Amazon, we've got you covered. Continue scrolling to discover the seven best exercise bikes, according to fitness editors and certified trainers, but first, a few things to keep in mind.

Being the Fitness and Reviews Editor of Men's Health means I get my hands on every piece of new gym gear that releases, sometimes before it's available to the public. To build this guide of the best exercise bikes, I took stock of the bestselling bikes on the market and put them up against overlooked classics and newly released options. I used each of these bikes for months, during long rides, interval training, as a warm-up, or a workout finisher.

Over the years, Men's Health editors like fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, CSCS, as well as key fitness trainers from both the MH Advisory Board and the MH Strength in Diversity Initiative, have taken great care in researching and evaluating the wide pool of exercise bikes. Their insights were also integral in creating this list. Our team also enlisted the help of Maillard Howell of Dean CrossFit in Brooklyn to test and assess how well these bikes performed in a real-world scenario (read: a high-volume, high-traffic training facility).

Ultimately, we ended up with the below guide that represents our top picks for the best exercise bikes we recommend buying now.

This new REP Fitness bike is one of the most impressive air bikes that our Fitness Director, Ebenezer Samuel, CSCS, has ever used. “The eight damper settings create a ton of options for my training,” he says. “And they essentially let the Strive simulate just about any air bike on the market.”

This Variable Pitch Resistance (VPR) system allows you to change the angle of the fan blades to give you that additional control over your workout. Most of us recognize the air bike for its impressive ability to make you suffer more the harder that you pedal it.

But with this new system, the Strive bike gives you the ability to use it for steady-state and recovery rides as well as those brutal finisher and max-effort sessions.

“At an 8, this feels like a serious strength-style quad-pump that's harder per pedal than a Rogue Echo or Scwhinn Airdyne,” says Samuel. “That versatility, and the speed with which you can switch between settings, opens a new world of fan bike workouts.”

The Strive is also as sturdy as they come among fanbikes. Underrated: The cupholder is everything you want on a bike, suitable for small plastic bottles and larger metal jugs alike (and great for your iPhone too).

Read more: How Our Fitness Director Overhauled His Garage Gym

This bike is no slouch when it comes to its hardware. In fact, it offers many of the same features as bikes double its price. From adjustable handlebars to convenient dumbbell holders, 32 levels of resistance, toe-cage pedals, and a spacious touchscreen display, it boasts all the features you'd expect from a premium bike. And it's wrapped in a sleek, matte black finish that oozes style.

The assembly is fairly easy, taking about 45 minutes to set up. The bike arrives in a box about 85 percent built with just the feet, handles, and pedals needing to be mounted. The bike has a nice compact frame and takes up the least amount of space in the home. The brand found a way to cut down on the cost by removing the expensive touchscreen and giving you a platform to place your own mobile device or tablet on, allowing you to follow any classes you want that way.

Whether we were sprinting or climbing out of the saddle during intense workouts, this bike offered unwavering stability. We never found ourselves wobbling or feeling uneasy on this machine. The elevated bullhorn handlebars aren’t just about style; they provide an elbow rest that we found essential during grueling climbs. It's a thoughtful touch that we found enhances overall riding comfort. The competition-style seat on this bike strikes a balance between comfort and support that other bikes we've tested in this price range miss the mark on. It's designed for long sessions, providing that Goldilocks level of cushioning.

This bike offers an all-around fantastic workout experience, especially for the cost, and is a great pick no matter your goals.

Read More: Best Workout Clothes, Tested by Us

The NordicTrack X24 earned its spot here because it doesn't just deliver cutting-edge training technology to the user, it also delivers the world for those more adventure-minded riders.

Do you want to feel like you are cycling through the Swiss Alps or along the beaches of Hawaii? That's what the NordicTrack offers, and not just by showing pretty landscapes on its 22-inch screen. Unlike the other bikes in this guide that simulate incline and decline by adjusting the magnetic resistance, this one actually replicates climbs or descents for a more accurate feeling of winding up and down the hills of Switzerland.

The setup is easy enough to do solo, but it helps if you have a person around to help you for some of the more involved moments of assembly. The bike is sturdy and robust, but also heavy, weighing over 200 pounds. So be sure that you build it in close proximity to where you are going to want to ride it, because it's not very easy to move around.

Like most NordicTrack equipment, the X24 comes with a year-long iFit membership.

Read More: Best Under Desk Bikes, Tested by Us

Looking for a bike that can handle years of punishment? Well, if the end of the world comes knocking, the Rogue Echo Bike will likely still be pedaling away. This thing is solid as a rock, capable of handling whatever you throw its way. This bike is a strength builder and a monster maker, demanding legit power from the user to make it move.

The bike arrives at your door well-packaged and about 80 percent assembled. The screen, arms, pedals, and feet are all that you need to mount. The process can take about an hour to get your bike ready to ride.

Its belt drive system is consistent and essentially maintenance-free, meaning you can focus on your workout, not tinkering around with the machine. We've used this bike on and off for more than seven years and have never experienced technical issues.

Beyond its durability, this bike rides like a dream. Pedaling is smooth and fluid in motion, and returns more resistance in response to the rider's effort.

We don't just recommend this bike; we swear by it. Years of firsthand testing have made it abundantly clear that if air bikes had Oscars, this one would be walking away with the golden statue, no doubt.Another unique feature of the Rogue Echo Bike is its fan blades. They’re crafted from thin-gauge steel, cut out and treated to a powder coating and welded to a central hub within the fan cage. While this level of detail might seem unnecessary, it adds to the fan's long-term reliability and overall aesthetic.

Now, we won't sugarcoat it; this machine is heavy. But fear not, because it comes equipped with wheels at the front. So, even if you're not the Hulk, you can still shuffle it around to ensure it fits in your workout space.

The only other qualm we have with this bike is that it doesn't feature multi-grip handles like other air bike models do.

There's a reason that Peloton's bikes have remained popular through the highs and lows of at-home training. The brand combines a superbly designed, connected bike with a charismatic crew of trainers and has ignited a fitness following.

The delivery is taken care of by Peloton, which unboxes and mounts the bike for you at your chosen location—an example of the high level of service that comes from the brand. The bike itself is fairly durable, and relatively simplistic outside of the 21.5-inch HD touchscreen. If you are interested in also utilizing the strength classes, consider Peloton's Cross Training Bike+, which comes with a pair of dumbbells and additional amenities like built-in Sonos speakers.

The truth is, when you buy a Peloton you are buying more than a bike, you are buying a community. And you are getting a best-in-class library of instructor-led, studio-style classes. From cycling to off-bike sessions like core workouts and hybrid strength classes, the variety of content is endless. The engaging live class offerings and fun gamification of your data makes the bike especially great for beginners or anyone who has difficulty maintaining a regimen.

On the downside, the initial investment can be a detractor, and the monthly subscription fee for access to Peloton's classes can also add up ($49.99 a month for all-access). However, if you fully utilize the platform and its library of classes, it can be a worthwhile expense.

The only other issue that our team had was instead of a traditional foot cage, the Peloton requires Delta-compatible cleats (which you can buy here) that clip into the pedals. It’s another step, and another purchase, before you start your ride. But this is to allow for the best experience with the programmed classes and resistance level changes, so it's an understandable ask. Peloton also offers a 30-day in-home trial with free delivery, setup, and, if needed, pickup and return for a small fee of $150. It's a commitment to customer satisfaction that helps protect your investment in this bike.

What we like about the Schwinn Fitness 290 Recumbent Bike is its excellent value. Sure, it doesn't have an overly huge touchscreen display, but that means you aren't tricked into spending your money on a bunch of technology you don't need. Plus, you can place your phone, tablet, or other device on the media shelf so you can ride along to on-demand, trainer-led classes with real-time coaching.

The seat's padding provides a lot of cushioning and ventilation, meaning you won't want to cut your workout short because you're uncomfortable. The weighted strapped pedals are also a win, because they feel secure on your feet so you can focus on your ride and not whether you’re going to lose traction.

The relatively lightweight build of this bike is also a positive, as anyone who has shopped for a recumbent bike understands. The majority of models are built like tanks, making them very hard to move around the house. The 290 also comes with rollers, making it even easier to transport.

How well this is built should be no surprise considering how long Schwinn has been in the bike game. This model is extremely comfortable for most sizes, including taller individuals, which is a nice improvement from the previous 230 version.

Read More: The Best Recumbent Bikes, Tested by Us

We understand that some of the bikes in this guide might be a little expensive for beginners who aren’t certain if they can stick with a new cardio routine or don’t need something that can handle intense workouts.

Merach is a brand that we’ve tested decent products from, and other pieces of its equipment have made our guides in the past. This design won’t take up much space, and it offers a good range of intensity through magnetic resistance.

Now, if you’re looking for an exercise bike that is going to take a lot of punishment and will handle whatever you throw at it, you might want to consider one of the more serious models above. This ride is only 62 pounds, which absolutely puts it on the lighter side and therefore prone to a little less stability if you’re really jamming on it.

If you’re looking for an easy-to-use bike from a trusted manufacturer, this is your best play for the money. And users on Amazon have commented on how quiet it is, with Merach putting it under 25 decibels in use.

In general, both devices can work for both high-intensity interval training workouts and long cardio. But how you prioritize your training will determine which bike is best for you. If you plan to focus on HIIT-based workouts, or want to mix weight training and bodyweight in with your bike work, the air bike is your jam. It’s easier to dismount the bike after a one-minute interval, then do a set of pushups, for example, making this ideal if you love CrossFit-style training.

If those steady-state workouts are your focus or you're using an indoor exercise bike to supplement outdoor riding, hop on a spin bike and get ready to pedal. And if you like doing cardio while watching TV, also opt for the spin bike; fan bikes are incredibly loud.

Everyone has different fitness levels and goals, but not every bike can cater to these needs. Look for a bike with adjustable resistance levels, allowing you to customize the intensity of your workouts however you choose. This is especially important if you're planning to evolve the intensity of your workouts in the coming months and years.

Magnetic resistance is often the smoothest, most predictable option, which is what you can find in most spin bikes. This allows bike makers like Peloton to seamlessly integrate the resistance level with its online classes. While air bike options like Rogue and Assault use traditional methods of wheel fans and friction to challenge your endurance.

The display should be easy to use and show all the essentials, including speed, distance, and time. Extra features like a heart rate monitor, Bluetooth connectivity, and USB ports can be nice to have, so decide if any of those are dealbreakers for you. For the higher-end spin bikes, the screens are more like full-service entertainment centers that can be used to watch a class with your favorite trainer or take you through a great cycling route in the Alps.

Some exercise bikes include preset workout programs, while others may offer a membership-based training app. Consider if having built-in programs is important to you, and if it is, if you want to consider a membership that would allow for a greater library of programs. There is typically a cost involved, so if you plan on using those features, be sure to confirm whether or not they are included. In some cases, these bikes might work perfectly without those higher-level subscriptions.

Consider the dimensions of the bikes you're shopping for, especially if you have limited space. Some bikes come with compact designs and foldable options for easier storage. If you'll need to stow the bike away when not in use, look for a lightweight frame on wheels.

Because exercise bikes are such a big investment, budget and warranty go hand-in-hand. Many of the bike models we recommend offer a solid warranty of between one and seven years on parts, plus at least a one-year warranty on labor. This is totally reasonable, especially if you're spending north of $1,500 on a new machine. You should expect that your bike's frame and resistance mechanism should both be warrantied for the life of the unit.

A 2019 study showed that regular use of an exercise bike—alone or combined with strength training—can have a positive impact on aerobic capacity in as little as three months. Researchers also concluded that, like other forms of regular cardio, using an exercise bike can also contribute to a reduction in systolic and diastolic blood pressure—especially when combined with a heart-healthy diet.

Indoor cycling is also a low-impact form of cardio, resulting in less pressure on the joints than activities like running or jumping. Cycling is not a load-bearing exercise, says Kate Heelan, PhD, a Professor of Research at the University of Nebraska at Kearney. This helps to minimize the risk of injury caused by malalignment, loss of balance, etc.

It's hard to beat the fresh air and sense of freedom an outdoor ride offers, but indoor cycling has a few unique benefits that can't be duplicated on the road. It starts with the fact you can hit hills or flat road when and how you want, just with the press of a button.

Because you'll never quite be 'coasting' or riding downhill on an exercise bike, indoor cycling workouts can allow you to get the benefit of several hours’ worth of outdoor riding done in half the time.

Cycling is an amazing way to get in shape and stay healthy, there's no doubt about it. But when it comes to choosing between indoor and outdoor cycling, what are the real differences?

"Broadly, both devices will burn calories and offer cardiovascular benefit," says MH fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, CSCS, "But they’re not the same experience."

For starters, there's the convenience factor. Indoor cycling is ideal for those who are short on time or live in regions where the terrain or weather may limit getting a ride outdoors. It can be much easier to hop on an indoor bike at home or at the gym and get a great workout without having to worry about traffic, the environment, or safety concerns.

Both modalities lead to cardiovascular benefits and, like any activity, they both burn calories. But outdoor cycling challenges other qualities, says Samuel. "You’re actively moving through space when you cycle outdoors," says Samuel, "which challenges your muscles to decelerate, react, and constantly micro-correct in ways it won’t on a stationary bike." The outdoor bike also offers a basic balance challenge that the indoor bike lacks.

Outdoors, the terrain and your pace set the challenge. Indoors, however, you can more easily customize your intensity, simply adjusting the resistance level on your bike. This (and the removal of the balance challenge) can make indoor cycling more beginner-friendly.

Many indoor bikes offer an interactive component now, like instructor-led classes and competitive leaderboards, which can be a great way to stay motivated and challenge yourself. These classes also provide a sense of community and support to stay motivated and hungry, even when you're working out alone.

Exercise bikes are generally considered to be a low-impact form of exercise, and they're a joint-friendly way to burn calories. But it's still important to take precautions to avoid the potential of injury. Here are some tips to help you stay safe on an exercise bike:

The 8 Best Weight Benches, Tested by Editors and Certified Trainers

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Expand your training possibilities at home with one of these picks.

A TRUE NECESSITY for any real home gym, a great weight bench opens the door to a ton of exercise possibilities. The best weight benches are sturdy and reliable, allowing you access to a whole range of exercises, and the ability to target nearly every muscle in your body.

The best weight benches can be used for way more than just the classics that bear its name, like the bench press. "They're good for everything from chest presses, dumbbell bench presses, incline bench supersets, skull crushers, glute bridges, incline rows to hit your back, ab moves, quad and leg moves like split squats, and more biceps moves than you can imagine," explains Men's Health Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, CSCS Neither flat nor adjustable weight benches occupy as much room as bulkier equipment, such as a hefty power rack, either. And for those that are adjustable, you can easily change the angle on your presses.

Whether you prefer to do your weight work with barbells or dumbbells (or even adjustable dumbbells), the best weight benches are compatible. Plus, many of the best weight benches include attachments and hidden features that allow for an even wider range of workouts, like a leg developer or squat rack.

We've gathered a selection of top-notch weight benches after rigorous testing and research to elevate your training and home gym experience. Keep reading, because you can't go wrong with any of these eight benches.

This model from Hammer Strength was originally introduced in 2016 exclusively for athletics and clubs, and was designed to be a versatile bench with an Olympic rack system. We're such a fan of this brand, we've got quite a few pieces in our own Men's Health gym. It's American-made and sold around the world due to its high-quality, durable construction.

With seven different back pad positions (from −10 degrees for decline lifts and up to 75 degrees for overhead presses), it packs more multi-functionality than most benches out there. Plus, it has built-in wheels, making it great for home gym spaces that require moving a bench around during workouts to accommodate space.

From Samuel's point of view, this is one of the sturdiest benches he's ever used. "Its tough surface lets me create a great base for bench presses," he says. An underrated thing he loves: the comfort of the seat portion of the bench, which allows for locking in a good position during overhead presses and incline curls.

It's more expensive than other options in this guide, but if you're looking for a bench that can keep up with your biggest reps and hardest lifts, this pick has got your back.

REP Fitness nailed it with this piece. This is the bench that I choose to lift with at home. The assembly is as easy as you could hope for with a heavy-duty bench like this. A few separate parts connect with screws and an Allen key. Everything was put together and ready for my workout in less than half an hour (just enough time for you to feel accomplished but not tired).

This bench shines in that it is as comfortable as it is durable. Few benches combine sturdiness, comfort, and versatility like this one from REP Fitness. It offers six different incline angles and a three-post design that anchors it to the floor no matter the exercise. It can also support up to 1,000 pounds, making it a solid option for novice to intermediate lifters.

"The backrest feels way more cushioned than other benches in its class, yet still manages to deliver rigidity when I drive my shoulder blades down for a set of bench presses," explains Samuel. This bench has only a minimal gap between the seat and back, which has never interfered with any of our lifts. It's also not ridiculously heavy (less than 100 pounds), so it's easy to move around my home gym when needed.

It's also a total breeze to adjust from fully flat up to a 90-degree incline. I've never had an issue with either of the two adjustment mechanisms getting stuck. Three seat positions add to its versatility, too. And the roller pads help secure your lower body with decline sit-ups and decline presses.

Bowflex's flagship 5.1S Collapsible Bench is a good mid-range weight bench for small spaces and beginners. What we love most about this bench is its stowable design—when not in use, it can be stored vertically to reduce its footprint by more than 50 percent. For those who only use a bench at home from time to time and aren't pushing hundreds of pounds per exercise, this transformable option is a great choice.

"This bench is rated to support up to 600 pounds, but keep in mind that it's considerably lightweight," says Samuel. "Meaning if you line up on there for, say, incline rows or preacher curls, it may slip. I find it works best for me for bench presses and seated exercises like press, biceps curls, etc."

The position of the leg bar is not perfect, especially for those moving heavy weight, and we found it can act as a roadblock to setting up lifts properly. That being said, there is a good number of exercises that you can do properly with this bench not possible with others. It features six adjustable settings, including 30, 45, 60, 90 degrees, flat, and more decline range than most similar benches we've tested (−20 degrees).

Flat benches often support more weight than their adjustable counterparts, but the REP Fitness FB-3000 tops the charts with the best weight capacity and comfort features of any flat bench we've tested. Grippy vinyl upholstery prevents slippage on its surface when setting up for a lift, and firm, high-density foam cushioning makes driving down feel as stable as benches double its price. There's even an optional wall hanger for stowing it away when not in use.

It stands 17 inches from the floor to the top of the pad, and features a three-post design equipped with wheels and a handle to make moving it around easy. Its dimensions are largely consistent with benches used in powerlifting competitions, ensuring powerlifting users a training experience that closely aligns with professional standards. Talk about setting up for success, right?

The FB-3000 distinguishes itself with incredible value, but it does come with the same limitations as any flat bench: You miss out on a range of exercises that incline positions on adjustable benches provide. A flat bench is great for most powerlifters, but limiting to the rest of us lifting at home. "It's obviously not going to do incline and is prone to sliding, so you definitely want a yoga mat under it." While it won’t be at the top of our recommendations for serious lifters (it will shift on you), it seems to be a decent budget option for beginners who aren’t sure how much they’re going to spend on home gym equipment. First and foremost, you'll need a weight bench strong enough to handle your training regimen—and even if you're not doing heavyweight moves, you'll want it to support a decent amount of load. That's because it's about more than your bodyweight and the weight you're training with; each time you set up on the bench, there's potential for additional impact. In general, aim for a bench that can support at least 500 pounds; that'll set you up for success in nearly all your training and support more advanced exercises that bring even more impact (such as step-ups and plyometric Bulgarians).

The right balance between stiff and cushioned padding is essential to ensure your bench will stay in good condition with use. Highly cushioned benches might feel nice, but they tend to deform over time, creating an uneven surface. This can also compromise your form during workouts, especially when your spine or shoulders are required to stay in alignment. While most padding is stitched to the bench, some is stapled, which tends to lift away from the bench’s frame eventually. Not enough padding isn't good either, as you'll feel like you're lying on a plywood board.

If you plan on using your adjustable bench for flat bench variations, be mindful of the distance between the back pad and seat pad (otherwise known as the ‘pad gap’). Anything larger than 2 inches will be noticeable and may feel uncomfortable during certain lifts.

In general, the three types of weight benches are flat, adjustable incline, and adjustable incline/decline. The first two are pretty self-explanatory and the latter, of course, adds versatility for decline-angle exercises. "If you're unsure which bench to get, opt for a bench with incline-angle options. Sure, this may cost slightly more initially, but it'll set you up with a bench that can grow with you," explains Samuel. "In general, most website workouts and app workouts assume that, if you're training with a bench, it has incline options. So not having incline options can set you up for frustration. It'll also heavily limit your ability to vary your workouts in fun ways. The incline bench is the most future-proofed purchase option."

If you're a powerlifter, or you're buying a bench for a particular program, a flat bench might be perfect. Decline-angle benches open up new movements (especially when it comes to core training), but they're generally unnecessary. Think of that as your luxury option.

Like all home gym equipment, weight benches can take up a lot of room. If you’re tight on space or just appreciate being able to move your equipment out of the way, consider a collapsible weight bench or, at the very least, one with wheels. If you’re after an adjustable weight bench, wheels should be a top priority. Because they’re on the heavier side, this will make stowing away and setting up tremendously easier. You don't want setting up your bench every session to be a part of your workout.

Return policies and warranties represent the manufacturer’s confidence in its product. And with most home gym equipment manufacturers, the better the quality, the better the warranty. In the case that something goes wrong with your bench, it’s important to know your investment is protected, so look for benches that come with at least a one-year warranty.

As the Fitness and Reviews Editor at Men’s Health, my job is to evaluate the latest and greatest training equipment available. I've had extensive experience with weight benches in both the training facilities I've worked out of and the home gyms I've built. I also consulted with Men's Health fitness experts, editors, and writers on the top weight benches they’ve experienced firsthand. The experts included our own Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. and Senior Fitness Editor Brett Williams, NASM. The team has tested dozens of weight benches over the years, from high-end, commercial-grade models to budget options.

Our extensive testing process involved a comprehensive evaluation of multiple factors—including size, durability, performance, suitability for different fitness levels, and value—to ensure that we recommend only the best. We tested each weight bench through exercise routines that included flat and adjustable bench moves, covering a wide range of motions and use scenarios. To determine stability and structural integrity, we tested benches at both comfortable and near-maximum capacities, allowing us to assess performance under varying loads.

To gather diverse perspectives, we consulted with our MH Advisory Board and Strength in Diversity trainers, who provided valuable feedback on the top weight benches based on their expertise and experiences. The result is the picks for the best weight benches you can buy right now.

You don’t necessarily need a weight bench to get strong and build muscle, but having one gives you access to a host of exercises (regardless of fitness level), especially when it comes to upper-body training.

Most basic exercises that beginners should master, like dumbbell presses and single-arm dumbbell rows and hip thrusts, are much easier to execute with a bench (especially an adjustable one). Meanwhile, more advanced lifters can get incredible mileage out of a bench by unlocking more innovative moves like half-bench single-arm presses and single-arm rows.

Here's a breakdown of the key differences:

A flat weight bench is designed with a fixed, flat surface, whereas an adjustable weight bench allows you to adjust the angle of the bench to different incline or, in some cases, decline angles.

"The adjustable benches that offer a decline setting are a terrific value for anyone who wants to get creative with their core exercises while training at home," says Samuel.

"Adjustable benches give you so many more options for your exercises," explains David Otey, C.S.C.S and Men's Health Advisory Board member. "Instead of just working at flat or upright, all of the angles in between can give you variety and diversity in your training options." Those angles allow you to bias muscle groups in different, more selective ways. A flat weight bench limits you to exercises that can be performed on a flat surface.

Adjustable benches offer more convenience overall (especially well-built ones), offering access to more movements and angles. Adjustable benches also offer support for extra movements, such as overhead presses and lateral raises, which can allow for greater loading of those movements in the long term. But flat weight benches win for convenience in two areas. They're generally lighter, so you can more easily move them around your gym, and they're easier to stow when not in use, too.

Because flat weight benches generally have fewer mechanisms and lack a hinge of any sort, they'll be easier to maintain. Adjustable benches don't require a ton of maintenance, but it's worthwhile every so often to check on bolts and caps, and make sure your hinge mechanism is working smoothly.

We consulted with Men's Health Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S., for the best exercises for beginners to get the most out of a weight bench.

The 8 Best Electric Razors for Men in 2026, According to a Grooming Editor

📅 Jun 13, 2018  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

WHOEVER SAID PATIENCE is a virtue did not have facial hair, or at least, they weren’t regularly shaving it. Even as Men’s Health’s Grooming Editor, I’m usually running behind and rushing through my shave routine, whether I’m packing my traveling grooming essentials, deciding what toiletries to bring camping, or just trying to get to work. If it weren’t for electric razors, I would have committed to a life of looking like Charles Darwin long ago.

Of course, there are plenty of reasons punctual guys might want to use an electric razor in lieu of traditional cartridge razors. These shavers are gentler on skin, gliding across the face with less direct blade contact to reduce irritation and minimize the risk of nicks and cuts. They also help prevent razor bumps and burns by trimming hair above the surface instead of dragging against the skin like traditional razors do.

To be clear, it takes skin a few sessions to get used to shaving with these devices if you’ve been a long-time cartridge devotee. This is because electric razors move differently and do not cut as close, so your skin needs time to adapt to the new sensation and technique. Also, switching razors changes how the blades interact with your skin, which can cause temporary irritation as your skin builds tolerance.

Still, these tools streamline the whole process: they're fast, easy to use, and travel-friendly since you don’t need to pack extra products, like shaving cream. Many models even come with smart features like pressure sensors and AI, making them more intuitive and unique to the user. Most you can use wet or dry, with or without shaving cream, and they're versatile enough for your face, head, or body. Plus, some have add-ons that can help you replace multiple grooming tools, simplify your routine, and free up space on your bathroom shelf.

But regardless of why you’re using an electric razor, the biggest thing is finding the right one for you. The good news is that no matter what your budget, skin or hair type, or grooming acumen, there's an electric razor that will suit your needs.

Braun electric razors have been a favorite of Men’s Health since we first launched our grooming vertical in 2018. For years, we maintained that the Braun Series 9 Pro was in a class of its own when it came to electric shavers. Then, the Braun Series 9 Pro Plus was released, which took these tools to an entirely new level.

At first glance, the Braun Series 9 Pro Plus looks almost identical to the original Series 9—and that’s not a bad thing. It keeps the same ergonomic design, which strikes a near-perfect balance in the hand: lightweight enough to maneuver easily, yet hefty enough to feel secure while shaving. Like its predecessor, the Pro Plus features five cutting elements that trim hairs down to an ultra-smooth 0.05 mm, giving you that just-shaved feel with impressive precision that’s as close as you’ll get without using an old-school barbershop blade.

Where it really starts to separate itself from the previous model, and any other electric razor for that matter, is the blades. The OG version’s blades were titanium, but the Pro Plus blades are made from surgical-grade stainless steel that stay sharper, allowing for a more effective shave that lasts longer. The Braun Series 9 Pro Plus, like the original version, features an ultra-thin ProLift blade (which looks like a gold bar on the razor head itself) that helps to lift hair away from your skin for a thorough cut.

What Else to Consider: Despite all that praise, the Braun Series 9 Pro Plus doesn’t lead the pack in terms of raw motor speed. It clocks in at 40,000 cutting actions per minute, which is solid but far from the top. But similar to Draymond Green’s career stats, the numbers don’t tell the whole story—this delivers the most powerful shave on the market. Sure, the Philips Norelco Shaver 9800 boasts a much faster motor (up to 150,000-cuts-per-minute), but speed isn’t everything.

What sets the Series 9 Pro Plus apart is Braun’s Sonic Technology, which adds 10,000 micro-vibrations per minute on top of the motor's output. These vibrations help lift and capture more hair with each pass, making every stroke more effective and efficient. The razor also features a pop-up trimmer and a flexing head, which performed especially well compared to the Panasonic Arc5.

Its pivoting head perfectly hugs facial contours with more precision, likely due to its slightly smaller, more agile design. It also includes the AI-driven ProSensoAdapt, which senses beard density 300 times per second, according to the brand. This automatically adjusts power in real time for a smoother, more personalized shave.

Why We Chose It: Shaving with the Braun Series 9 Pro Plus rarely takes more than five minutes, even less when my stubble is light, but its standout feature is the incredibly close shave it delivers. The innovative focus is on lifting hairs more effectively from the skin to ensure a smoother finish.

Another feature I didn’t know I needed from an electric razor is the ProComfort pre-shave head, which gently lifts and prepares hairs for a closer, more comfortable shave. It does this by using fine, vibrating blades to raise and align hairs before the main shaver cuts them.

These upgrades make a real difference. It’s by far the closest shave I get from an electric razor, especially when I use the ProComfort head beforehand to prep my skin. No other tool gets me this close to 5 o'clock before the shadow shows up.

Long-Term Results: I’ve been using my Braun Series 9 Pro Plus for over a year, and it still shaves just as well today as it did right out of the box. Overall performance has not changed, though there has been a slight decline in cutting power when the blade dulled. But replacing the head was easy and brought the shaver back to business immediately.

Another Men’s Health shaving staple, especially for those with sensitive skin. You’d expect a razor this powerful to cause irritation, especially considering its five (incredibly sharp) blades, but it doesn’t. The Arc5 shaves nearly as close as any manual razor and cream combo I’ve used, and the pivoting head effortlessly glides over tricky areas like my neck and jawline without missing a spot.

Despite its impressive performance, the device feels light in the hand and operates super quietly; it’s barely loud enough for you to realize it’s on. While it doesn’t match the Braun Series 9’s extras like AI-powered shave sensors that adjust to beard density, I’ve still found this simpler version more than capable. It gives a clean, close shave without any fuss.

What Else to Consider: With a wide head and a strong motor, this shaver is powerful. It boasts 55,000-cuts-per-minute—more than the Braun Series 9 Pro Plus but less than the absurd Norelco 9800 150,000-cuts-per-minute motor. Even if you don’t have sensitive skin, go easy until you get the hang of how much to press the foil against your face.

Like Davante Adams in the middle of the field, the Arc5 cuts fast. Still, don’t rush through shave jobs, as this can cause some irritation if you’re not careful, no matter the device you’re using. Once you get the hang of it, though, it minimizes the amount of time you have to shave the same area. Overall, it is slightly bulkier than the closest similar razor (Braun Series 9) and has a shorter battery life (45 minutes versus 60 minutes), which could come down to the fact that it’s just a larger device in general.

The Panasonic Arc5’s blades are hypoallergenic, as they’re made from surgical-grade stainless steel that resists corrosion, minimizes skin reactions, and maintains a smooth, low-friction surface, all of which makes it ideal for dealing with sensitive skin. Additionally, the foils covering the blades are designed to be gentle and smooth, which further helps protect the skin from irritation or razor burn.

Why We Chose It: In addition to convenience, the biggest draw of electric razors is that they’re much gentler on skin, helping avoid the razor bumps and irritation that come with close manual shaves. What stands out to me about this model is how closely it shaves—nearly as close as a traditional wet razor, yet without the usual irritation.

After the few shaves it took for my skin to adapt, I noticed a significant drop in bumps. It’s a powerful, efficient tool that’s straightforward and intuitive with no unnecessary features to complicate things. For anyone with sensitive skin, this razor is easily the most skin-friendly option I’ve tried, and it delivers smooth results without the harsh aftermath.

Long-Term Results: The Arc5 holds up over time. After months of use, the cutting power has not decreased a bit, and post-shave irritation remains a rare occurrence. The blades are as sharp and gentle as they were out of the box.

For anyone constantly battling stubborn neck hairs, this is the razor for you. The Norelco 9800 brings AI into the shaving routine to deliver an impressively close and precise shave. Its rotary heads easily follow facial contours, while built-in pressure sensors give real-time feedback, letting you know when they’re applying too much or too little pressure.

Powered by Dual Steel Precision blades, which are two layers of self‑sharpening stainless steel blades that increase cutting speed and efficiency, making up to 150,000 cuts per minute. This razor doesn’t leave a single stray hair behind.

With 72 rotating blades offering full 360-degree movement, it seamlessly adapts to every curve of the face. To top it off, the smart companion app tracks shaving patterns and offers personalized tips for better technique, making this one of the most advanced grooming tools on the market.

What Else to Consider: The Norelco 9800 blows every other rotary shaver out of the water. Not only is the motor by far the most powerful (again, 150,000-cuts-per-minute!), but the three-head design offers a great range of motion, allowing for more pivoting and a closer shave around the contours of your face. The blades on the 9800 also have a micro-bead coating to help reduce irritation, which we found helpful.

Though not usually a huge tech junkie, I recommend using the app to change the settings, especially if you use it wet or dry, because adjusting those settings manually can be difficult. That being said, it does take a bit of time to adjust to your style of shaving. It’s a bit overly responsive at first, so when you first apply more pressure in less sensitive areas, like your cheeks, it can overcompensate when moving to areas like the curves in your neck and lead to nicks. However, it quickly adjusted after a couple of uses.

Similar to Braun’s AI, the 9800 senses hair density and automatically adjusts; however, it also provides real-time pressure feedback via the light ring and connected app.

Of all the rotary-head razors I’ve tested, this one flexed and pivoted to the shape of my face the best without missing too many spots. My favorite thing about the Chairman Plus is how streamlined the design is. Sure, there are a handful of nifty gadgets and features, like the travel lock, SkinSafe blade protection, and ergonomic grip, but none are excessive. More than anything, however, they’re all functional.

So often with grooming devices, they tend to sound exciting or interesting, but when put into practice, they’re either overly complicated or straight-up unnecessary. That’s not the case with The Chairman Plus, as every feature was clearly designed to simplify the shaving experience and make it as easy as possible.

What Else to Consider: Of all the practical design boons of this trimmer, the most notable is also the simplest: its grip. The blade type is a key factor to consider when choosing an electric razor. You’ll typically choose between two main options: rotary blades and foil blades.

If you usually let your stubble grow out or don’t shave every day, a rotary electric razor is your best bet. Its circular motion and flexible head follow the curves of your face, helping it cut through longer or more uneven hair with ease. Rotary razors work especially well for those with normal to tougher skin and medium to coarse beard density, offering a comfortable and efficient shave even after a few days of growth.

A foil works best for shorter facial hair or when you're aiming for a close shave similar to classic razors. It uses oscillating blades hidden beneath a thin, perforated foil that captures and cuts hairs as they emerge through the mesh. While a foil shaver tends to be noisier than rotary models, it offers deeper precision. To get the smoothest results, use it with straight, steady strokes—moving back and forth along the face and neck. This design makes foil razors ideal for those who shave daily or want clean, sharp lines around facial hair.

Corded razors plug directly into a power source, so you don’t have to worry about charging or replacing batteries. As a result, they deliver more consistent power and longer performance. Since they don’t need to conserve energy, they also usually run stronger than cordless models. That said, cordless razors offer added convenience, especially for travel or grooming on the go, with options powered by either rechargeable or disposable batteries. Ultimately, the right choice depends on your routine and how much flexibility you need.

Many premium models offer additional features or accessories to enhance the shave experience or make it more convenient, including:

The Men’s Health grooming team has been extensively testing electric razors since 2018. For years, we’ve been putting shavers to the test, evaluating their effectiveness, durability, and ease of use over long periods of time. As someone who can get a five o'clock shadow before lunch, I am constantly shaving. This has given me a consistent canvas to test and retest editor favorites, as well as the latest innovations and new grooming releases.

For this story, I considered the team’s reviews and notes of top-performing razors dating back eight years, in addition to my own experience, to land on 15 options to extensively test or retest over an 18-month trial period. I’ve also included before-and-after shots of yours truly for most of the razors to show the kind of shave each one delivers. Based on this first-hand use, I narrowed that list to eight, representing the best electric razors for men to buy in 2026.

Both electric and manual razors use blades to cut hair, but they do it in different ways. Manual razors pull each hair up with the first blade, then slice it closer to the skin with the following blades—kind of like using a knife. Electric razors, on the other hand, work more like scissors. Most use a foil system: hair enters tiny holes in the foil, and internal blades cut it from beneath. Rotary versions use a slightly different mechanism, but the concept is similar.

Manual razors shave closer because the blades touch the skin directly. That closeness comes with a downside: higher chances of razor burn, bumps, or ingrown hairs, especially if you skip proper prep or use a dull blade. Electric razors always have a barrier between the blade and skin, so while they won’t shave quite as close, they’re often gentler and better suited for sensitive skin.

Read More: 2026 Men’s Health Grooming Awards

Using an electric razor is much easier to pick up compared to a traditional or safety razor. Still, don’t just grab yours and start shaving right away. Take the time to learn how to get the closest shave possible before speeding through the process for the best results.

Start your shave with clean skin. Whether you’re using a wet or dry shaver, you should tackle your scruff right after washing your face. For dry shavers, apply a small amount of pre-shave oil or wash your face and then gently pat it dry. These shavers perform best when the stubble is firm and completely dry.

Keep the razor at a 90-degree angle to your skin to avoid cutting hairs unevenly. There’s no need to press hard, as a light grip does the job. Pressing down too much, especially around the jaw or chin, can lead to patchy results and irritation. With a foil shaver, I stick to back-and-forth strokes. If I’m using a rotary, I’ll go with either circular or straight motions, depending on what feels best.

Reading the manual might seem unnecessary (and boring), but brands often include specific tips or techniques tailored to their design. Those little details can make a big difference, resulting in a noticeably smoother shave. It might not be Hemingway, but taking a few minutes to go through the instructions usually pays off in better results and a more comfortable experience overall.

As long as I stick to proper maintenance and care, I get a consistently smooth shave from electric razors. If you’re new to these tools, however, give your skin about two weeks to adjust—it makes a big difference. A little patience and upkeep go a long way.

One of the first things I check in an electric razor is how easy it is to clean because this will help both its effectiveness and longevity (not to mention my blood pressure). Skipping regular cleaning can clog the blades with hair and gunk, which not only affects performance but also shortens the razor’s life. When using a waterproof device, I always rinse mine under running water after each use, then use a small brush to clear out any buildup on the foil.

That foil is key to getting a smooth, even shave, but it’s delicate. If it gets bent or damaged, replace it right away. Letting it go risks rough shaves, irritation, or even breakouts from dragging dirt across your skin. Some higher-end models, like the Braun Series 9, come with automatic cleaning stations. They’re super convenient (even if a bit bulky and noisy) and use replaceable cartridges that last around three months—about $5 each in a multi-pack. While not essential, they definitely make it easier to stay on top of maintenance.

Read More: How To Maintain Your Beard Trimmer

High-quality electric razors—especially those in the $200-plus range—can last years, sometimes even five or more if well maintained. To keep mine running strong, I make sure to replace the blades and foil regularly. Depending on the brand, blade replacement is usually recommended every 6 to 18 months. I also keep an eye on the foil. If it gets dented or damaged, I swap it out immediately since most replacement cartridges include both parts.

These typically cost between $20 and $50. Battery life is typically the first thing to go. Over time, even the best razors start to lose their charge faster or run with less power. When I notice my shave isn’t as close, or the razor starts pulling instead of cutting cleanly, that’s my cue that it might be time for a new one.
